<meta>This page continues the philosophical reflections of Louis-Claude de Saint-Martin, likely transcribed from a collection of his shorter works or letters. It transitions from a spiritual defense of the human soul to a critique of Enlightenment political theory.</meta>

*Letter to a friend on the French Revolution*, ( page 6 ).
One should above all observe that the soul of man can only live through admiration <note>Saint-Martin uses "admiration" in the classical sense of "wonder" or "awe"—a spiritual recognition of the divine</note>, and that among all other beings <margin>ecstasy of the admirable</margin> in nature, he alone is capable of it; that this need for admiration in man presupposes a source above us, an inexhaustible source of this same admiration which is our most essential nourishment. Without this, our very origin would have deceived us by forming us with a commanding appetite that it could not satisfy. This demonstrates at once, with a single stroke: the existence of a supreme and admirable Being who lives by his own admiration; the absolute superiority we hold over all beings in nature, since we are the only ones who can participate in the sweetness of this admiration; and finally, our immortal relationships and our powerful analogy with the eternal and inextinguishable hearth of life and light.

*Flash on Human Association*, ( page 17 ). He (Rousseau) <note>Jean-Jacques Rousseau, author of "The Social Contract," whom Saint-Martin frequently critiqued for focusing on human conventions rather than divine origins</note> examines, like all other political writers <note>original: "publicistes"</note>, the laws and conventions that, according to him, these two faculties (liberty and will) have made or should have made; whereas he only needed to examine the laws and conventions they should have followed. One should have said to these faculties: *know the immutable laws and conventions that exist before you; fulfill them; and then the social order will be in its proper proportion.*

For it would be an unheard-of novelty in every class and every series of beings if there were even one who was sent by its source into a region where it had to create the laws according to which it—

<insert>Divine intelligence creates the Intelligible —</insert>

<summary>In these excerpts, Saint-Martin argues that the human capacity for wonder proves our divine origin and critiques Rousseau's political theory by suggesting that social order should be discovered in eternal laws rather than invented through human will.</summary>
<keywords>Louis-Claude de Saint-Martin, French Revolution, Jean-Jacques Rousseau, admiration, wonder, Supreme Being, social order, Divine Intelligence, spiritual philosophy, Enlightenment critique</keywords>

...should live; where he would not find those laws already established, and where he would have anything else to do but to conform to them: an axiom <term>axiom: a self-evident truth that requires no proof</term> that we do not fear to submit to the examination of the most severe observers, and which teaches us in advance the value we should place on all those laws that the political man manufactures for himself every day.

*Reflections of an Observer on the question of the Institute: What are the institutions best suited to found the morality of a people?* (page 49.) If it is for the legislator to communicate the "spirit of life" to his nation, and if he must first be impregnated with it himself; how could he become impregnated with it, if he did not feel within himself the desire and the strength to penetrate to the very sources where this fire resides? And how would he feel this strength and this desire, if he did not begin by removing from the hearth that must receive this spark all the foreign substances which, even if it appeared, would prevent it from catching fire?

The enjoyment and communication of such a treasure would undoubtedly hold the first rank among the rights of man; and that is why men move with such ardor toward the high positions where this sacred fire is supposed to reside: but, like all rights, it is given, it is obtained, it is received, and it is never usurped.

<margin>Buffon <note>Georges-Louis Leclerc, Comte de Buffon (1707–1788), a famous French naturalist whose work Saint-Martin critiques here for being too focused on the physical surface of nature.</note></margin>
degree of the series of things, at which they begin to be, and that of those degrees at which they have the power to emerge from themselves, and to manifest themselves, ..... like a flower is the visible reunion of all the properties that exist invisibly from its root up to itself ..... It is for this reason that <term>sensible objects: things that can be perceived by the five senses</term> so greatly stimulate our curiosity; thus, it is less what we see in them, than what we *do not* see in them that attracts us ..... This is why when the <term>naturalists: scientists who study the physical world and living organisms</term> strive to charm us with the elegance with which they describe what is visible and palpable in these sensible objects, they do not fulfill the role they seemed to have taken upon themselves with regard to nature ..... They deceive our expectation, by not satisfying in us this burning and pressing need which carries us less toward what we see in these sensible objects, than toward what we do not see in them.

<meta>This page continues a discussion regarding the "moral sense." The author is responding to an interlocutor who likely accused him of only writing for a religious audience. This page begins mid-sentence.</meta>

moral sense; if you seemed to fear that I were working only for those who are religious, you should be reassured by the principle I have explained to you. For it is clear that I would also be working for those who might wish to be <term>impious<note>In this context, "impious" refers to those who actively reject religious devotion or traditional piety.</note></term>, given that it is only through this same <term>moral sense<note>The author uses *sens moral* to describe an innate human capacity to perceive right and wrong. He argues this faculty is active even in those who reject religion, as one must have a moral compass even to intentionally act against it.</note></term> that one can be either one or the other.

I repeat to you that no one is more tolerant than I regarding the choice each person is free to make in this matter, and that no one has ever felt less of a passion for <term>proselytism<note>The act of attempting to convert others to one's own opinions or religious beliefs.</note></term> than I. But I cannot help but point out the disadvantage you have brought upon your own cause by failing to probe deeply enough into the foundations upon which you believe you can base your defense.

Indeed, the entire world is composed of only two classes of men: those who are religious (among whom I include all those termed <term>idolaters<note>A historical term used here to categorize those practicing non-monotheistic or "pagan" religions; the author includes them in the "religious" class to show the breadth of his argument.</note></term>); and those who are the opposite, who are impious and atheistic. As for the indifferent and the neutral, they are only non-entities because their moral sense is dormant; and as soon as it should wake from its slumber, it will immediately take a side, for or against.

Thus, by defending the moral sense, from whatever angle it is viewed, I am working for the whole of humanity; while you, by excluding the sense...

<summary>The author argues that the "moral sense" is a universal human faculty required for both religious devotion and atheism, asserting that his philosophical defense of this sense serves all of humanity. He positions himself as a tolerant observer who avoids the urge to convert others, while criticizing his opponent for a shallow understanding of human nature.</summary>
<keywords>moral sense, religious tolerance, proselytism, atheism, indifference, human nature, philosophy of religion</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author's critique of an opponent's argument regarding the "moral sense." Using a satirical "reductio ad absurdum," he demonstrates that if one assumes human morality and religion are merely products of physical means or social systems, one could logically—and preposterously—attribute the same hidden complexities to animals. This follows a discussion on the classification of men into the religious and the impious.</meta>

( 80 )

moreover, since they possess all these means, that they would not make the same use of them among themselves as we do; one could not know that there were no conversations among them about **spirituality**, and about **religious opinions** just as there are among us, as well as those **scholastic** and **philosophical** discussions on high matters that occupy us so much and with so little fruit; and since we are in the position of making suppositions regarding these **animals**, I maintain that one could no more know that they would stop at simple conversations on these objects, and that they would not have, like us, the temptation to convert into acts and ceremonies all these opinions that they would stir up in their discussions.

Thus, I might say to each of my adversaries: it is impossible for you to pronounce on what these animals do in this regard <term>without your knowledge<note>The original French "à votre insçu" (modern: à votre insu) suggests things happening "behind one's back" or beyond one's perception.</note></term>. For just as they are ignorant of what happens among us in this matter, so we may well be ignorant of what happens among them. Thus, at the moment when you are traveling peacefully on your horse, at the moment when your dog catches a quail, and your cat a mouse, perhaps some are in a <term>Sorbonne<note>The historic theological faculty of the University of Paris, used here as a metonym for rigorous, perhaps overly pedantic, academic debate.</note></term>, others in the pulpit, others performing the duties of <term>dervishes<note>Original: "derviches." Referring to members of Sufi Muslim ascetic orders; the author uses the term to represent "exotic" or varied religious roles to highlight the absurdity of his hypothetical.</note></term>; etc.

That is how reckless suppositions lead us imperceptibly to the **absurd**. I return to my subject.

When you see me take up the defense of the

<meta>This page continues the author's philosophical argument from page 80. The previous page ended mid-sentence discussing various groups (theologians in the Sorbonne, preachers in the pulpit), and this page picks up by identifying the common thread between them: the moral sense.</meta>

( 81 )

**moral sense**, if you seemed to fear that I was working for those who might be religious, you should be reassured, according to the principle I have explained to you; for it is clear that I would also be working for those who might wish to be <term>impious<note>In this 18th-century context, "impious" (impies) refers to those who actively reject or mock religious tradition, rather than just being indifferent.</note></term>, given that it is only with the same **moral sense** that one can be either the one or the other.

I repeat to you that no one is more tolerant than I regarding the choice that each is free to make in this matter, and that no one has ever had less than I the passion for **proselytism**. But I cannot refrain from pointing out the disadvantage you have given your own cause by not probing deeply enough into the foundations upon which you believe you can support your defense.

Indeed, the entire world is composed of only two classes of men; one of those who are **religious** (among whose number I also include all those called <term>idolaters<note>A historical term used by European writers to categorize all non-monotheistic or "pagan" religions.</note></term>); the other, of those who are the opposite, or who are **impious** and **atheists**: for the **indifferent** and the **neutral** are nonentities only because their **moral sense** is numbed; and as soon as it wakes from its slumber, it will immediately take a side, for or against.

Thus, by defending the **moral sense**, from whatever angle one considers it, I am working for all of humanity; and you, by excluding the **sense**

<meta>This page continues the author's argument from page 81, where he asserted that the "moral sense" is the essential foundation for both religious belief and its opposite. He is responding to an interlocutor who appears to have attacked religion by highlighting its historical "horrors."</meta>

( 82 )

**moral**, you set the whole of the human race against you—that is to say, not only those who wish to be religious, but even those who wish to be impious and atheists; for you deprive them of the only ingredient with which they can be satisfied.

If by chance, instead of the fear that I might raise up those idols of which you speak (on page 24), you feared that I might overthrow them, you see that you would still have cause to be reassured regarding me, and that I would not need to use my own hand for this demolition; for it would be you, primarily, who would cast the first stone at these idols, or who would cause their temple to crumble by suppressing the only foundation upon which it can stand.

<margin>+</margin>
You would therefore have proved nothing by painting for us the abominations and horrors that <term>religious matters<note>The original French "la chose religieuse" refers here to the outward practice, institutions, and historical conduct of religion, rather than its internal spiritual principle.</note></term> have spread across the earth: abuses do not invalidate principles; they confirm them. We only misuse that which actually exists. You would surely not reject an ancient medal without having carefully examined what might be hidden under its rust and its <term>verdigris<note>original: "verd-de-gris"; a green pigment or crust that forms on copper or bronze through oxidation. It is used here as a metaphor for the distortions of history and dogma covering an underlying truth.</note></term>. And although in our cities and in our countrysides, one encounters all too frequently abominable arsonists who, in their fury, consume our houses, our furniture, and our properties, often making us the prey of the flames ourselves, nonetheless you would not only never dream of trying to teach us that fire does not exist, but even all these ravages will not prevent you from warming yourself with pleasure,

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's response to an interlocutor who has pointed to the historical crimes of religion as proof of its falsehood. The author uses the analogy of fire—useful for cooking yet dangerous in the hands of an arsonist—to argue that the misuse of a principle (like religion or the moral sense) does not negate its underlying truth.</meta>

( 83 )

and that you are not very glad that there is fire for cooking, for the arts, and for the other needs and pleasures of life: the first instruction given to us by the <term>science of human understanding<note>A term popularized by Enlightenment thinkers like John Locke; it refers to the systematic study of how the human mind acquires knowledge and distinguishes truth from falsehood.</note></term> is that it must not be error that makes truth flee; but that, on the contrary, it is for truth to make error flee.

Another important instruction that this science of human understanding must give us is that the <term>moral sense<note>The internal faculty that allows humans to perceive right and wrong, similar to how the physical senses perceive light or sound.</note></term> can, like all our other faculties and like our bodily senses, be universal without being universally developed.

For the word "universal" may express only a universality of existence, and not a universal activity, and even less an activity that is uniform. It is thus that one can say that language is universal among men, although they do not always speak, and above all although they make such different use of their languages, whether in form or in substance.

These are the first elements of <term>regular philosophy<note>The author uses this term to describe a systematic and disciplined way of thinking that adheres to fundamental principles of nature and logic.</note></term>. If you had recalled these elements, you would not have been stopped by the furies of the <note>original: "prêtre fanatique"</note> fanatical priest, nor by the atrocities of the savage, nor by those of the civilized man.

I say: if you had recalled these elements, for I am well persuaded that they are not—

<meta>This page continues the author’s rebuttal regarding the "atrocities" mentioned at the end of page 83. The author is addressing a critic who uses the historical failings of religious or moral people to argue against the existence of a universal moral truth. The author uses a scientific analogy—the different states of matter—to explain how a single truth can appear in different forms or stages of development.</meta>

( 84 )

foreigners. Indeed, how could you have undertaken to instruct us on the great subjects that occupy us without being equipped at least with this knowledge? It would be as if someone who was ignorant of the different states of freezing, liquefaction, and gas to which the same substance can be reduced, wanted to teach physics.

But if you are not ignorant of these elements, as I am persuaded you are not, perhaps you—seeing me only as a student—do not believe me advanced enough to have reached them; and that, consequently, you do not think it appropriate to conduct yourself with me as if this knowledge were common to us both. I therefore feel obliged to assure you that for more than thirty years I have been deeply imbued with these principles; consequently, you would have committed no imprudence by dealing with me on this basis.

Thus, to dispel these difficulties that terrify you, let us remember that the <term>moral sense<note>The internal faculty described by Enlightenment philosophers that allows humans to distinguish right from wrong naturally.</note></term>, though universal, may not be universally developed.

It is the same with this tiresome question of <term>innate ideas<note>The philosophical theory that the mind is born with certain concepts or knowledge, rather than being a "blank slate" at birth.</note></term> (or lack thereof), which echoes continually in the <note>original: "écoles"</note> schools <note>A reference to "Scholasticism" or traditional academic institutions where logic and theology were debated.</note>, and which you also bring forward, following Descartes, Plato, and Malebranche (*pages 26 and 29*); it has long been irrevocably decided for me, and I would like not to im—

<meta>This page continues the author’s response to a critic regarding the origins of human knowledge. The previous page ended with the author beginning to address the "tiresome question" of innate ideas and scholastic disputes that the critic had raised.</meta>

( 85 )

to involve myself again in these scholastic disputes; nevertheless, I am going to follow you there.

You note, quite rightly, a great difference regarding the point in question between Plato and Descartes.

By not neglecting this positive impulse, perhaps you would have reached the conclusion, which would have been to completely purify Platonism and, above all, to protect it from the exaggerations of <term>Malebranchism<note>The philosophy of Nicolas Malebranche (1638–1715), who famously argued that "we see all things in God," suggesting that God is the only true cause of our perceptions and thoughts.</note></term>.

The **spiritualists** <note>In this 18th-19th century context, "spiritualists" refers to philosophers who believed the mind or soul is a distinct substance from the body, rather than the modern association with communicating with spirits.</note> who, as I have informed you, are not at all from the class of professional theologians and metaphysicians; who are very tolerant of the opinions of others, although they take extreme care to guard themselves against them when they believe them to be false; who do not transform a discussion into an accusation; who, finally, do not treat a man's doubts as a crime, as you fear (*page 42*); the spiritualists, I say, are specifically and invariably opposed to the <term>ideologues<note>A school of French thinkers (like Condillac or Destutt de Tracy) who argued that all human ideas are derived solely from physical sensations.</note></term>, who would have us create our ideas *from* our sensations, whereas ideas are only transmitted to us *by* our sensations.

They do not believe, like Descartes, that our ideas are <term>innate<note>The theory that certain concepts, such as God or mathematics, are hard-wired into the human mind from birth.</note></term> within us.

They do not believe, with Malebranche, that God himself produces all our ideas within us.

Although they have major differences with Plato—which would be too long to explain here—it is

<summary>The author distinguishes his "spiritualist" philosophy from other schools of thought, arguing that while sensations transmit ideas, they do not create them. He distances his view from Descartes' innate ideas and Malebranche's divine intervention, seeking a middle ground in the interpretation of Plato.</summary>

<keywords>Plato, René Descartes, Nicolas Malebranche, spiritualism, ideology, innate ideas, sensations, scholasticism, metaphysics</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s explanation of the "spiritualist" perspective on the origin of ideas, specifically contrasting their views with Plato while acknowledging their proximity to his thought. The previous page discussed the distinction between these spiritualists and the "ideologues" who derive ideas solely from sensations.</meta>

( 86 )

...however, it is with him <note>Referring to Plato.</note> that they have the least disagreement, while rejecting, nonetheless, both his <term>metempsychosis<note>The philosophical term for the transmigration of the soul, or reincarnation, especially after death.</note></term> and all those opinions attributed to him, which one cannot help but recognize as bizarre—at least when judging them by the light in which they are presented to us.

They believe that our ideas are not at all innate *within* us, but *beside* us.

They believe that our mind is born and lives in the midst of thoughts, just as our bodies are born and live in the midst of the elements and all the productions of nature.

<margin>identical</margin>
They believe that our mind can gather around it, if I may put it this way, all those various ideas by which it is surrounded, and which are its most essential nourishment; just as we gather, through the organs of our body, all the fruits of the earth that our body cannot do without.

They believe that if there were not in our mind a foundation <note>The original word *base* implies a structural or chemical prerequisite for interaction.</note> analogous to the ideas by which it is surrounded, they could never penetrate it, nor form any alliance with it; just as if there were not in our stomach juices analogous to the juices of earthly fruits, these fruits could never, within our viscera, be transformed into <term>chyle<note>A milky fluid of droplets of fat and lymph that is formed in the small intestine during digestion and passed into the blood.</note></term> and blood.

They believe that the health of our mind depends on the harmony we are able to maintain between this foundation...

**Vocabulary:** metempsychosis, innate ideas, mind, elements, chyle, blood

<summary>The author explains a spiritualist theory where ideas are not stored inside the mind from birth (innate) but exist around it like an environment, comparing the mental absorption of thoughts to the physical digestion of food.</summary>

...which characterizes it, and those ideas upon which it exists in a state of continual dependence; just as the health of our body depends on the harmony we manage to maintain between the digestive juices of our stomach and the nutritive juices of the earthly fruits which are absolutely necessary to us.

And, although I only provide these purely elementary accounts with regret, I nevertheless present this simple summary to you, as it may help to clarify—

The first consequence to be deduced from all that has just been laid out is that if the moral sense, though universal, is not universally developed—even if it allows itself to be altered and corrupted by a false <term>regimen<note>Continuing the metaphor from the previous page, "regimen" refers to the "diet" of ideas and habits that nourish the soul.</note></term>—we should not be surprised by all the false results that will arise from it. # Debates. Volume III.
### G

...through which we carry out the deliberations occasioned by our deep moral affections.

If, then, this **moral order** is truly closer to us than the **physical order**; if we are, in a way, charged with its **administration** <note>In this context, "administration" refers to the stewardship or management of our own character and ethical life.</note>, it would not be surprising if we were also to abuse it more. Indeed, I see quite commonly that the hand of man, when it is not infinitely prudent, spoils everything it touches.

On the contrary, since we do not approach the **principles of nature** as closely; since they possess a hidden law which is not at our disposal; and finally, since our power in this regard only disrupts their *results*, then nature itself remains fundamentally responsible for administering the bases of our **physical senses**.

Now, since nature tends with all its might toward regularity, and since we cannot interfere in its governance—whereas it is at once our right and our duty to interfere in the moral government, which is our own—it is not surprising that if **monstruosities** <note>As noted previously, "monstrosities" refers to any deviation from the natural or intended "type" of a being.</note> creep into the productions of nature (which is not committed to our administration), these monstrosities are so few in number compared to those seen in the moral order, which is at our disposal.

And truly, if we had the "great hand" over the physical order as we do over the mor[al order]—

# Debates. Volume III.
### G

...[mo]ral order, I believe that nature would have felt its effects long ago; and that the **monstrosities** it would offer would be as numerous and as inconceivable as those we see arising in our **moral order**. Perhaps they would even go to such an extreme as to make us one day deny even our **physical senses**; and I would not be surprised if the **system of molecules** <note>The author is likely referring to the materialist philosophies of the Enlightenment, such as those of Diderot or d'Holbach, which suggested that all reality—including thought and morality—could be reduced to the movement of physical particles or "molecules."</note> were to lead us one day to this bizarre theory.

Let your just indignation and your surprise extend, then, as far as you please over the furies of **fanatic priests**; over the monstrous atrocities of **savages** and their barbaric practices; over other little-known monstrosities because they are seldom spoken of in books; over even the cruelties of nations reputed to be **civilized**; and finally, over the apathetic numbness into which men almost generally allow this **universal moral sense**—which I defend—to fall; you will not be right, for all that, to rise up against it <note>The author argues that human corruption and apathy do not disprove the existence of an innate moral compass; they only prove how much we have neglected it.</note>.

Indeed, you could look at all these examples as being merely different modifications of one and the same base: just as a volcano sometimes releases from its depths salts and sulfurs that are useful for trade and agriculture; sometimes it ravages and completely overturns the lands <note>The original French text is trimmed at the edge here; "lands" is inferred from *contrée*.</note> by which it is surrounded; and sometimes it has intervals of rest during which it gives no sign <note>Trimmed text: *signe*.</note> of existence. For if we see some of these volcanoes cease <note>Trimmed text: *cesser*.</note> altogether, we also see new ones being born.

Do not, therefore, lean so heavily, I implore you, on the objections behind which you entrench yourself, since they remedy nothing; and because facts everywhere confirm the base you attack, and nowhere destroy it.

itself; and you see here to what extent I carry this assurance, since in taking a stand against you in favor of the **moral sense** and the **religious principle** <term>chose religieuse: Literally "the religious thing." I claim, first, that if the proofs alleged against **divinity** by the philosophers are so frail and weak, those that its partisans use for its defense are no less so when they only know how to prove this divinity through the spectacle of **nature**; as is their daily custom, and, so to speak, their only response. The reason for this is simple.

Nature is a mixture of **disorder** and **harmony**, of evils and goods. It has two faces: if, by one, it proves the wisdom and power of a superior principle; by the other, it would prove not only the impotence of this principle, but even its wickedness. In short, it is a witness always ready to testify for or against at will. Now, in sound **jurisprudence** <note>The author uses a legal metaphor here, treating nature as a witness in a trial regarding God's existence.</note>, one cannot form a solid judgment based on such contradictory depositions; and one has the right to demand a witness who is more fixed and more in agreement with himself.

...and loving, but they often ended up seeing God so much in His representative that when the minister was not there, there was no longer any God for them.

Finally, through a series of new abuses, the idea of God and that of the priestly robe <note>original: "robe sacerdotale." This refers to the external vestments and institutional authority of the clergy.</note> have become so linked that they no longer appear without one another; so that whether it be the vast geniuses or the simple men, they can no longer hear mention of God without being immediately haunted by this priestly robe, which comes to take His place in their minds. This causes the former to deny Him with indignation and contempt, and the latter to spend their days in a despairing terror of never being able to reach God without the approval and authorization of His representative.

If to this picture of passive evils that the minister of religious matters <note>original: "la chose religieuse." This phrasing suggests the administrative or institutional side of religion.</note> has spread upon the earth, one wishes to join that of the active evils of every kind which he has likewise allowed to be introduced or which he has enacted himself, one will see the obstacles accumulate. One will not be surprised that on one hand, philosophers and vast geniuses feel their indignation grow when the divinity presents itself to them only in the image of its minister, who—like human representatives—so often puts himself in the place of his principal <term>principal: "commettant" in French, referring to the person who grants authority to an agent or representative. In this context, God is the principal and the priest is the agent who has exceeded his mandate.</term>; and on the other, that so many simple and gentle people have come to no longer want this divinity, whom the minister manifested only in such repulsive colors.

But if I have regretted that a man of talent like...

<meta>This page continues the author's address to intellectuals who have rejected religion due to the failures of its human representatives. ->( 101 )<-

you, were stopped regarding the **moral sense** <term>sens moral: a concept popular in 18th-century philosophy, suggesting that humans have an innate internal faculty for perceiving moral truths, much like the physical senses perceive light or sound.</term>, by difficulties that rested only on imaginary terrors, I would no less regret seeing you persist in these hasty conclusions of which I have just painted a picture; and that you did not take advantage of the circumstances that the revolution has brought about around you.

<margin>Year of our Lord 1795.</margin>
Indeed, whether the **priestly robe** <note>original: "robe sacerdotale." This refers to the external vestments and institutional authority of the clergy, which were stripped away during the secularizing waves of the French Revolution.</note> owed its disappearance only to natural and political causes, or whether it owes it to a providential hand that wished to cause its departure—so that this sign no longer presenting itself to us, the **divinity** might reclaim in our thoughts the rights that this obstacle caused it to lose—this priestly robe has nonetheless disappeared; and it seems, in the midst of the world, to no longer exist except in another world.

However, if it were true that those who wore and who still wear this robe were indeed under the weight of a **providential justice**, I would not go so far as to ask you to make your opinion yield to the sympathy their situation might inspire; although it would always be proper to have for them the respect due to them, both by the nature of their punishment and by the dignity of their judge: but I would ask you to look at them as no longer existing for you; and I would want you to take advantage of this favorable opportunity to reconcile yourself with your **moral sense**, regarding which this priestly robe may have frightened you more than you think; I would want that, through the consistent study you might undertake...

<summary>Writing in 1795, the author suggests that the removal of the clergy's institutional power during the French Revolution was a "providential" opportunity for people to find God directly through their own moral sense. He encourages readers to look past the fallen priests and focus on their own internal spiritual growth.</summary>
<keywords>French Revolution, priestly robe, moral sense, providential justice, secularization, religious institutionalism</keywords>

->( 102 )<-

regarding this **moral sense** without recurring either to books, or to traditions, or to any of those suspicious testimonies of which I spoke above, you should try to give yourself a new account of all these questions—which you have not sufficiently separated from the **uniform** <note>original: "l'uniforme." Not only would I believe that this path could lead you to clear solutions regarding these matters; but I am convinced that without it, you will never succeed in resolving any of these questions upon which I have felt able to go so far as to offer you my profession of **certainty**.

If you do not adopt this proposition, as I have reason to believe you might not, at the very least you cannot reproach it for being insufficiently **philosophical**. Indeed, is there anything more philosophical than a method that isolates you from all the outrages of **fanaticism**, from all the extravagances of **superstition**, and even from all the obscurities—not to say, from all the daydreams—of **metaphysics** <term>metaphysics: in the 18th century, this often referred to abstract philosophical speculation about the nature of existence that was not grounded in physical or internal experience.</term>?

<margin>Transition</margin>
For, in urging you to suppress all traditional books whatsoever—which, on one hand, rely on the **moral sense**, and on the other, serve universally as the basis for religious matters—I would equally urge you to set aside all the books of the metaphysicians and the famous writers under whose banners you align yourselves; in short, all books whatever they may be and without exception: so convinced am I that the complete cultivation of your **moral sense** will make known to you, in this order of things, everything that it will be necessary for you to know!

I concede, nonetheless, that if the method I propose to you is highly **philosophical**, it is at the same time very drastic <note>original: "violent." In this 18th-century context, it implies a radical or extreme intellectual departure from conventional thought.</note> and very exclusive; and this is what made me doubt whether it would suit you.

However, you cannot accuse it either of leading to that applause and that glory whose echoes have, according to you (page 33), prevented **Rousseau** <note>Jean-Jacques Rousseau (1712–1778), the Genevan philosopher whose ideas on the inherent goodness of man were highly influential yet controversial.</note>, **Hutcheson** <note>Francis Hutcheson (1694–1746), a Scottish-Irish philosopher who argued that humans possess a natural "moral sense" through which we perceive virtue and vice.</note>, and so many others, from seeing the reef upon which they had foundered. I can speak of this from experience; this has not stopped me from walking this same line since I began to think, nor does it stop me today from being more attached to it than ever.

I must also warn you that with the principle I defend, one is less exposed than you think to those applications which you claim (on that same page 33) are never precise, and are easily oratorical and poetic. I am too frank to hide from you that you have, in few words, painted a faithful portrait of your own philosophy—which indeed requires the arts of **eloquence** <note>original: "l'élocution."</note> and, in this regard, finds a useful support in your **talents** and your skill.

My cause is such that even if I were to defend it with the greatest negligence of style, and even with clumsiness, I would still win it; given that it is so strong in itself that it defends itself almost on its own. Whereas with yours, no matter how much you might exhaust all the secrets and treasures of eloquence in its favor, it could not fail to escape you; and you should expect to lose it.

As for me, I presume, as far as I am permitted to judge, that the system of these two writers offering me, on one hand, bases that are proven *true* to me, and on the other, effects that are proven *good* to me, must be closer to the **inner sympathy** <note>original: "intima sympathia." Otherwise, whichever side triumphs, all my wishes are for the truth.

--- Page 40 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's philosophical critique of his correspondent's "system of sensations." On the previous page, the author questioned whether the correspondent's work could truly defeat the "moral sense" theories of Hutcheson and Rousseau by reducing morality to physical impulses.</meta>

->( 106 )<-

...the two words above, *triumphant* and *victorious*, submitting my opinion in advance to those who are better positioned than I to decide upon them. I certainly believe that one can triumph without conquering—humanity gives us proof of this every day—but I also believe that one can only triumph over a thing insofar as one can conquer it. Now, since truth cannot be conquered, it follows that one cannot triumph over it either; one may well triumph over those who do not know how to defend it, but one does not triumph over truth itself for that reason.

In short, one triumphs over an enemy or an obstacle because they can be conquered and overcome. But truth is not an enemy seeking our ruin; it is not an obstacle that stops us; it is not a weak understanding that can be dazzled or led astray; therefore, we can never conquer it, nor triumph over it. Your expression has a brilliance that might seduce; but, pending a better opinion, I believe your idea was not yet mature.

Let us return to the radical <note>original: "parti violent" — meaning an extreme or drastic course of action</note> proposal I make to you: namely, to abandon all books whatsoever. I see that you have already partially acquiesced to my opinion on this point. For if you dispense with the **moral sense** <note>The innate capacity to distinguish right from wrong, which the interlocutor seeks to replace with a purely physical "system of sensations."</note>, then with even greater reason must you have cut out that vast number of books dealing with divine and religious matters, which relied solely on this moral sense. Indeed, of what use is a building once its foundation has been removed?

<meta>This page continues the author’s critique of the recipient's philosophical stance. <sig>H 2</sig>

You have even pushed industry and courage much further in this regard than the fox of the fable <note>A reference to Aesop’s "The Fox and the Grapes."</note> did. Seeing that he could not reach the grapes, he contented himself with saying they were too green. As for you, who find yourself in the same situation as him, you have improved upon his presence of mind by saying that the grapes did not exist; and that it was a folly to seek them, since there were none at all.

But in urging you toward the extreme course <note>original: "parti violent" — meaning a drastic or radical resolution</note> of which I have just spoken, or toward guarding yourself against all traditional books and all codes of religious matters, my objective would be for you to succeed in resolving the difficulties, and not for you to take the resolution of cutting through them <note>The author suggests the recipient is simply ignoring problems rather than solving them.</note>; it would be that, like an opulent and active landowner, you might seek within your own domains whether there might not be some richer mine and some purer metal than those brought to you amalgamated with so much foreign earth; it would be that, in rejecting far from you all the sacred empirics <term>empyriques sacrés: a derogatory term for religious authorities who treat spiritual matters with the superficiality of medical quacks</term>, you would obtain positive proof of their charlatanism; finally, it would be that you could draw from your own depths the specific <term>spécifique: a 18th-century medical term for a remedy uniquely suited to a particular ailment</term> that they all claim to give you, and of which each of them proclaims himself, without proof, the custodian.

Now, how would you have found these solutions with them, since they are still, in this regard, in a state of trial and error <note>original: "tâtonnemens" — suggestive of stumbling or groping in the dark.</note> and research; since they are in a state of perpetual discord among themselves, and since you yourself find it necessary to be on guard against some of the opinions of the authors you follow?

That course of action was therefore less philosophical than the one I propose to you. And in truth, I see nothing less philosophical than a study of human understanding <note>original: "cours d'entendement humain" — a likely reference to the "Essay Concerning Human Understanding" style of philosophy popular in the 18th century.</note> which relies only on books. I also find that, when one is as rich as you are in one's own thoughts, it is not valuing oneself enough to resort to the thoughts of others.

But, 1st, it seems to me that the true character of a philosophical doctrine is to give the greatest possible latitude to the human spirit, and not to reduce it to such a limited enclosure; to let him feel and exercise all the faculties that constitute him, and not to chain and suspend them: for medicine has never taken the state of numbness <note>original: "engourdissement" — a state of torpor or lethargy.</note> in which opium places us for our true bodily health, in which we can develop all our movements and all our activity.

<meta>This page continues the author’s critique of the recipient’s refusal to commit to either a materialist or spiritualist philosophy. The previous page ended mid-sentence, questioning the recipient's "profession of faith." This page completes that thought and challenges the recipient to define what actually governs human thought if both matter and spirit are rejected.</meta>

<page-num>( 111 )</page-num>

[...to in]struct, it would be good to put those who listen in a position to know exactly what to think of the **fundamental doctrine** of the one who teaches.

If, in the realm of divine and religious matters, we have seen that all the men of the earth comprise only two classes—those who believe, and those who do not believe—it is equally certain that in the ranks of the <term>system-building philosophers<note>original: "philosophes à systêmes." This refers to thinkers who create rigid, all-encompassing theoretical frameworks to explain reality, rather than relying on empirical observation.</note></term>, there can be only two sides. One side insists that matter is our sole **motive force** <note>original: "mobile." The underlying power or cause that sets human thought and action in motion.</note>—a doctrine I find impossible to distinguish from **materialism**. The other side recognizes that we are **spirits**, and as such, we possess our motive force within ourselves; this force is what I have called the **moral sense**. All philosophical systems, whatever they may be, consist of one or the other of these elements, or else a combination of both.

So, if you will have neither matter nor spirit, I leave it to you to teach us to whom you attribute the **governance of our thought**; for there must, after all, be one.

As for me, who could not reconcile myself to such an ambiguous position, I shall be so bold as to provide here a history of your own thought.

You are a spirit who has not delved deeply into either of these two systems, and who, at first glance, was easily repelled by both: by materialism, because it offered no demon—

Without realizing it, you always took on some shades of their respective colors, from which you gradually composed for your thought this indeterminate and mixed way of being. This is the state of a great many men on earth; it cannot satisfy you, despite all the efforts you make to fill the voids, and sooner or later, it will be shaken—and that by itself—even though you claim (on *page 44*) that nothing can shake your foundation.

I even believe that you will lean toward the **system of the spirit** rather than toward the **system of matter**. For, if it is less difficult for a <term>materialist<note>A person who believes that nothing exists except matter and its movements and modifications.</note></term> to rise to the class of the spiritualist than for a spiritualist to descend to that of the materialist, then this upward movement will be all the easier for someone who, like you, without being a spiritualist, has nonetheless protected himself from materialism.

I admire, however, how you have guaranteed your safety from materialism while positioning yourself as you...

Nothing there repels me; everything attracts me. Although <term>Bacon<note>Francis Bacon (1561–1626) was an English philosopher and statesman often called the father of empiricism for his influence on the scientific method.</note></term> leaves me with many things to be desired, he is nonetheless, for me, not only less repulsive than Condillac, but even a hundred degrees above him. <meta>This page continues the author’s argument that Francis Bacon’s philosophy supports a spiritual worldview, unlike the "sensationalism" of Condillac. Having just introduced Bacon as a preferred authority, the author now quotes him to demonstrate a balanced view of religion and superstition.</meta>

<page-num>114</page-num>

the meaning I give to this word is as much in my favor as it is against yours. I am certain I would have been understood by him <note>Referring to Francis Bacon.</note>; and I have reason to believe I would not have been understood by <term>Condillac<note>Etienne Bonnot de Condillac (1714–1780), a French philosopher who argued that all human knowledge stems from sensory experience.</note></term>. Bacon certainly cannot be accused of approving of superstition, since he says (chapter XLII, part one): *Ignorance and barbarism introduce superstition; hypocrisy sustains it with vain ceremonies; false zeal spreads it, and self-interest perpetuates it.* But as a profound and measured philosopher, he says in the same chapter: *the excessive fear of superstition leads to a disadvantage almost as dangerous as superstition itself.*

Bacon thus seems to me to have held the scales with exact balance across all the subjects he submitted to his observations; he did not say everything, undoubtedly, regarding the delicate points that you and I are discussing today—either because he lacked certain data, as I believe, or because he was held back by reasons of prudence, as neither <term>d'Alembert<note>Jean le Rond d’Alembert (1717–1783), a French mathematician and philosopher who famously praised Bacon in the "Preliminary Discourse" of the *Encyclopédie*.</note></term> nor anyone else has doubted. But one must believe that he thought everything he said, because nothing forced him to say it; furthermore, what he put forward in favor of religious matters did not sufficiently offset what he had expressed against their abuses, and therefore would not have protected him from anything.

This is why I found, with pleasure, in his work, a hundred features and even entire chapters (notably the 33rd, on Virtue, part one) where I recognize my most essential foundations, and where yours are almost always either challenged or corrected.

<summary>The author defends Francis Bacon as a balanced thinker who critiqued the abuses of religion while recognizing the dangers of extreme atheism. <meta>This page continues the author’s philosophical investigation into the nature of "revelation." Having previously warned the reader to set aside books and traditions to focus on the self, he now seeks to reclaim and redefine the term from its narrow religious associations.</meta>

<page-num>118</page-num>

also a little the word *revelation*; and, in advance, I beg you to do me the favor of not believing that I confuse this word with *books* <note>The author is likely referring to "Books" in the sense of Holy Scripture or established religious dogmas.</note>.

Commonly it applies only to a so-called direct communication of the divinity with a few men, to whom it is supposed to share its will, and the general or particular plans it has decreed, and which encompass either the divine and spiritual laws it comes to prescribe to us, or the events relative to the fate of nations, or the revolutions of nature.

But it has also received, in the social dealings of men with one another, a less elevated meaning, and one which is more within their reach: it is thus that when someone communicates to me knowledge that is particular to them, I can say that this person reveals to me what was a secret to me; it is thus that we say every day: nature reveals its secrets to those who study it.

In our languages, this word therefore applies in general to the manifestation of a hidden thing, of whatever order it may be.

But as long as a hidden thing remains such, that is to say, as long as it is not communicated or revealed, it will not be known: and as soon as any thing whatever has been known, I can conclude from it that it has been communicated or revealed; for it is here that I extend the word *revelation*.

<note>original vocabulary: revelation, divinity, divine laws, nature, secret, manifestation, communication</note>

<summary>The author redefines "revelation" by stripping away its exclusively religious context, arguing that any act of learning or uncovering a secret—whether from God, another person, or nature—is a form of revelation.</summary>
<keywords>revelation, divinity, divine law, nature, secrets, manifestation, communication, epistemology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author’s philosophical argument from page 118, where he began to broaden the definition of "revelation" from a strictly religious term to a general process of discovery. <page-num>119</page-num>

And even, after having expanded the word, I believe I can expand the fact, and regard all the treasures of nature—the fruits of plants, the chemical <note>original: "chymiques." This archaic spelling reflects a period when the study of chemistry was still emerging from its alchemical roots.</note> properties of various mineral substances, the laws of the movement of bodies, the phenomena of light, of electricity, etc.—as making their own revelation toward us; that is to say, as communicating to us through their manifestation the knowledge or the secrets which concern them, and which, without this manifestation on their part, would have remained as if non-existent to us. It is thus that a rose, in developing, manifests or reveals to my eyes all the treasures that were hidden, first in its germ, and secondarily in the bud; and regarding these treasures that plants reveal to us, see how many wonders naturalists have discovered enclosed within the breast of the cotyledon <term>cotyledon: the primary leaf of the embryo of a seed-bearing plant; the "seed leaf" that provides nutrients to the germinating plant.</term>.

Thus, the whole of nature can be considered as being in a continual, active, and effective revelation; or as ceaselessly making its own revelation, according to all the degrees and all the classes that constitute it.

Having thus considered the physical order, if we wish to consider the intellectual and moral order, we must recall what we have said previously; namely: that man is born and lives in the midst of thoughts.

Those of these thoughts that reach his center cause a fermentation <note>original: "fermentation." Since matter does not think, not only could it not have imagined for itself either the substance or the form of these phenomena it presents to us in the interesting class of polyps <note>The "polype" was a central subject of 18th-century debate. <meta>This page continues the author’s critique of materialism and the limitations of sensory-based science. This is why physicists and all those who concern themselves only with the sciences of matter so easily believe that everything is matter.

This is why, despite the opposition you declare against materialism, you have nevertheless been led to say, like its followers, that you strictly knew nothing universal except for the universe (page 32); while there is something in you far more universal than this universe—namely, your thought.

They circumscribe themselves within the revelations of matter; they pay no attention to the developments occurring within man, or to the natural and proper revelations that take place in our being by means of that atmosphere of thoughts that surrounds us.

Those who occupy themselves with man, but who restrict themselves to exercising only the faculty of reason within themselves, likewise believe in nothing beyond their reason, because they live only within the developments or revelations of reason. They are, however, in an order of things far superior to that of the revelations...

--- Page 66 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's philosophical exploration of the different levels of human understanding. Picking up from page 127, the text concludes a thought about how those who rely solely on reason exist in a realm that is superior to the physical world, but still limited.</meta>

<page-num>( 128 )</page-num>

of matter, which cannot reach up to them. Let a geometer <note>original: "géomètre." In this period, a mathematician or surveyor who applied abstract principles to physical reality.</note> who exists within the revelations of reason, assure simple villagers who exist only within the revelations of matter; let him assure them, I say, that he is going to measure exactly the height of a steeple, or the length of a field, without approaching either one, they will not believe him: and even when he has convinced them by the fact itself, they will not understand it.

But reason is only the torch of the thinking man; it is not his life: there is in him a faculty even more radical <note>original: "radicale." From the Latin "radix" (root), meaning a faculty that is fundamental or rooted in the very core of human nature.</note> and deeper; it is the one you do not want; it is the one to which we have already given so many names, and which celebrated writers have called before me, the <term>moral sense</term> <note>original: "sens moral." A key 18th-century concept referring to an innate spiritual or ethical faculty that allows humans to perceive truths beyond logic or physical senses.</note>.

Now, this radical faculty, this moral sense, must also produce its own development or its own natural revelation, without which it will not be known. As long as it does not produce this development or revelation within us, our being is not complete, nor is our knowledge, because we paralyze or cut off one of our foundations, and one of our centers of development; and without this foundation, without this radical center—in short, without the active play of our moral sense—we can no more, through our reason alone, achieve the developments or revelation of knowledge analogous to this moral sense, than we can, through the simple developments or revelations of matter, achieve the developments or revelation of knowledge analogous to our reason.

<vocab>moral sense, reason, matter, revelation, geometer, radical faculty, developments</vocab>

<summary>The author uses an analogy of a surveyor to explain that just as physical senses cannot grasp abstract mathematics, human reason alone cannot grasp the "moral sense." He argues that this spiritual faculty is a fundamental part of human nature that must be activated to achieve complete knowledge.</summary>
<keywords>moral sense, reason vs matter, radical faculty, epistemology, revelation, enlightenment philosophy, Louis-Claude de Saint-Martin</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author's argument from page 128, where he established the "moral sense" as a fundamental human faculty. He is now refining the definition of "revelation," distinguishing between the personal, internal manifestation of truth and the secondary, traditional revelations found in religious texts.</meta>

<page-num>129</page-num>

I have reluctantly touched upon this important point, which would require being treated *specifically for this purpose* <note>original: "ad hoc." Latin for "to this," meaning a treatment dedicated solely to this particular topic.</note>, and which, on its own, would be the subject of the widest speculations. However, I will easily forget the restraint I placed upon myself to speak to you about it, if, in the little I have presented to you on this subject, I have been able to lead you to see the word *revelation* in a light other than that which it holds for the common folk <note>original: "le vulgaire." This refers to the uninstructed public or those who take terms only at their surface value.</note>; and if I have been able to show you that, by using it with me, you have not disconcerted me at all.

Indeed, far from reducing the meaning of this word, as common opinion does, I show that everything which develops in nature, in our reason, and in our <term>moral sense</term> <note>See previous page; the innate faculty for spiritual truth.</note>, manifests itself in each of these classes only by its own positive *revelation*. This is in contrast to the revelations contained in the books and religious doctrines of all the nations of the earth, which are merely traditional *revelations*. These not only require the intervention of man to be transmitted, but their certainty can only be effectively demonstrated to you through your own faculties, and by placing yourself in the same state as those who are said to have been the subjects and heroes of these *revelations*.

For however much physical science may recommend experiments, true religious science recommends them far more; and it is because so few people take the trouble to perform this verification <note>The author suggests that spiritual truths should be tested and experienced personally rather than accepted blindly.</note> that there are so many living in blindness and error. Let us move on to the subject of languages.

If I have seemed to shield myself from the efforts that you see...

<meta>This page continues the author’s response to a previous argument, likely defending the spiritual or innate nature of language against a more materialistic or "sensationalist" view that would reduce speech to a mere system of physical signs. He transitions from discussing "revelation" to redefining "language" itself.</meta>

<page-num>130</page-num>
<header>( 130 )</header>

<margin><unclear>Signs</unclear></margin>
...[you see]med <note>The French text begins with "bliez," likely the end of "sembliez" (seemed), continuing from the previous page's discussion of the interlocutor's philosophical approach.</note> to do to reduce languages to the name of signs, it is because, not having then determined the meaning of your expressions, I feared that you would bring us back, once again, by that means, into that narrow circle of sensations <note>The "narrow circle of sensations" refers to Sensationalism, a popular 18th-century philosophy (associated with thinkers like Condillac) which argued that all human ideas and languages derive solely from sensory experience. The author views this as a restrictive view of human nature.</note>, to which I believe the language of man to be very superior. And even though today you define these same expressions more clearly, if I looked very closely, perhaps you would not have entirely dissipated my fears in this regard.

Nevertheless, since what you say currently can apply to my principles as well as to yours, not only will you see how little I fuss over words, but even that I shall not fear to extend the terms *signs* and *languages* even further than you, just as I have done with the word *revelation*.

Thus, I can, like you and like those who first said it, admit that all signs are languages, and that all languages are signs; (*page 41*) and even my conviction on this is as old as the one I expressed to you previously on this proposition: that a sense can be universal and not be universally developed. I will therefore have no trouble showing you the idea I attach to the word *language*; and here, I will draw from a work published some time ago a portion of what I am about to present to the reader.

<margin><unclear>Signs</unclear></margin>
I believe that a language, in its broadest sense and at the same time in its most rigorous sense, can be regarded as the manifest expression of the pro- <note>The text cuts off mid-word, likely continuing as "propriétés" (properties) or "progrès" (progress) on the following page.</note>

Thus we possess all three kinds of languages, namely: that of non-animated material beings, through our mere corporeal existence; that of sensitive beings, through our cries and the acts of our instinct, both of which serve us in expressing our animal affections <note>In this context, "affections" refers to physical feelings, drives, or emotions common to animals, such as hunger or fear.</note>; and finally that of intelligent and loving beings, through the power we have to paint with our speech everything that pertains to the play of our ideas and the coloring of our moral sentiments.

<meta>This page continues the author's critique of how the word "convention" is misunderstood in the context of the origin of language, further distinguishing his and Rousseau’s views from both purely human social contracts and supernatural miracles.</meta>

<page-num>( 141 )</page-num>
<sig>K 3</sig>

Men are so accustomed to instituting, their minds are so identified with their conventions and with their own works, that by the word **institution** <note>In the 18th-century context, this refers to the act of establishing or founding something, whether by nature, God, or social agreement.</note>, they conceive of nothing, absolutely nothing, except what they institute themselves: and so, if it happens that a man less led astray than they naturally gives to this expression the real significance it ought to have, they do not understand him at all; they reject him with disdain; and such is the sad fate of truth, that when it tries to break through the midst of the languages of men, it finds almost not a single word there that has not been debased <note>The original French word is *abâtardi*, suggesting a corruption or loss of the word's original, "pure" meaning.</note>.

Rousseau's idea, to which mine is entirely similar, does not therefore concern any kind of convention regarding the institution of speech.

Nor does it concern any kind of **miracle**; a word that you no doubt use by design, and which you would perhaps not be sorry to see me use as well, so as to start from there to turn against me both this **priestly robe** <note>A metaphor for clerical or religious authority. The author is likely responding to an opponent who wants to frame his philosophical argument as mere religious dogma.</note> and those revelations to which I am very far from having referred you, as you well know.

It concerns absolutely the pure and simple necessity that, by our nature as a thinking being, we have received—along with our intellectual and moral life—the germ of the analogous language <note>The idea that language is not an external tool, but a biological and spiritual "seed" (germ) that corresponds to the structure of human thought.</note> that is proper to it; just as animals receive with life, the germ...

<summary>The author argues that language is a natural, necessary outgrowth of human thought rather than a human invention or a supernatural miracle, critiquing how social conventions corrupt our understanding of truth.</summary>
<keywords>Jean-Jacques Rousseau, origin of language, institution, revelation, thinking being, priestly robe, natural necessity, debasement of language</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author's argument regarding the origin of language, moving from the rejection of "convention" (discussed on page 141) to the concept of a generative source that transmits the "seed" of speech to the human mind, much like biological traits are passed down in the animal kingdom.</meta>

<page-num>142</page-num>

...cries and sounds related to the needs of the animal order: for, just as the animal that engenders commonly possesses all the properties that must one day manifest in its offspring, so too must the generative source of our thinking being <note>The author uses "thinking being" (*être pensant*) to distinguish human consciousness from the "animal order."</note>, whatever it may be, possess all the properties that this thinking being is expected to develop in its time.

<margin>The Word</margin>
Now, as this development reveals in us the eminent property of speaking, and above all of speaking of moral and intellectual things of which the animal has no idea, it is natural to think and to say that the source in question must not only have sown in us the seeds <note>Original: *germes*. In 18th-century philosophy, this refers to latent potentials or biological blueprints.</note> of this kind of speech, but that it must, beforehand, be provided with it itself, just as we see that we are provided with all the properties that we transmit to our children; all these are natural and fundamental truths, expressed energetically by this beautiful and sublime proposition: **speech was necessary for the institution of speech.** For here, the "institution" is nothing other than the transmission of the seed on the part of the source, whatever it may be, and the progressive development on the part of the engendered being—excepting the varieties that this development may offer in the non-thinking region <note>Likely referring to the physical or material world where speech is articulated.</note> where it must operate.

I see you, from here, besieged by the scarecrow of consequences <note>A metaphorical "bogeyman" or "straw man"—the opponent's fear of where this logic might lead (likely toward a divine or transcendental origin of language).</note>, and by the numerous difficulties for which your system will only let you perceive the inconveniences, and never the remedies. But which must prevail: a system that is false, or—

<summary>The author argues that human speech cannot arise from nothing; rather, it must be a developed "seed" transmitted from a generative source that already possessed the capacity for language. ...of a system that is obscure, and which will even cease to be so for you, once you have activated that **moral sense** <note>The author frequently emphasizes that understanding higher truths requires an internal moral faculty, not just cold logic.</note> I have never stopped telling you about? As for me—who, as I have already told you, do not take it upon myself here to give a lecture on these matters—I leave you to grapple with the phantoms by which I see you surrounded; and I will, once again, tighten my focus <note>Original: *serrer la mesure*. This suggests narrowing the argument or proceeding with greater precision.</note> by showing you why I cannot, as you do, attribute Rousseau’s beautiful and sublime proposition to mere oversight.

His oversight <note>Original: *inadvertence*.</note> and his misanthropy had indeed led him to view the **wild state** as the true **natural state** of man, and to derive from among savages that **social contract** <note>A reference to Jean-Jacques Rousseau's influential 1762 work *The Social Contract*, which posits that political authority relies on an agreement among individuals.</note> which never existed except in his own mind.

Now, since he had gone so far as to draw this **social contract** and this source of **civilization** from that wild order, it would have cost him no more to seek there also those **languages**, or that <term>speech|Original: *parole*. In 18th-century thought, *parole* often refers to the actual faculty or power of speaking, distinct from specific *langues* or languages.</term>, which would have been no harder for him to invent than it is for you and the other founders of languages; and which perhaps, in itself, is less astonishing than civilization and all the accessories of a functioning social contract would be, if we were to suppose they were produced solely by the hands of men.

<summary>The author critiques Rousseau's "Social Contract," arguing that if one assumes man could invent complex civilization from a primitive state, then inventing speech would be equally possible—yet the author maintains that both actually require a higher source.</summary>

--- Page 86 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's critique of Jean-Jacques Rousseau's paradox. The previous page established that Rousseau viewed the "wild state" as natural, yet struggled to explain how humans could have invented both a social contract and the language required to form it without one preceding the other.</meta>

<page-num>( 144 )</page-num>

It seems to me, therefore, that to solve the problem Rousseau presents us with—by wanting, on one hand, to make man the inventor of his social contract, and on the other, to make the origin of his languages dependent on a word that he makes an indispensable condition <note>original: *sine quâ non*</note>—we must take a position that is at once more accurate and more advantageous.

Whatever wonders the genius of Rousseau may have brought forth, and whatever reproaches one may have for his heart—since I believe this famous man died less insane in his head than in his heart—I am convinced that, naturally, his heart was worth more than his head. Nevertheless, he sometimes fell into the trap into which those who devote themselves exclusively to the profession of writer fall every day; and this trap consists of them often being driven to make their heads work much more than their hearts.

Now, speculations concerning the origin of our human associations, and the fundamental basis of the social contract, belong much less to the heart than to the head; especially when one sees in this contract only a human basis, as Rousseau did.

This is why, in undertaking to treat this subject, he was able to err, and lead us into the land of chimeras <note>In this context, "chimeras" refers to idle fancies or impossible dreams, derived from the mythical Greek monster made of disparate parts.</note>, as have all those throughout time who, without relying on principles superior to man, have tried to build the edifice of human association and civilization.

<summary>The author argues that Rousseau's theories on the social contract are flawed because they rely solely on human logic ("the head") rather than spiritual or "superior" principles. This reliance creates a logical impasse regarding whether language or society came first.</summary>
<keywords>Jean-Jacques Rousseau, Social Contract, origin of language, human association, civilization, philosophy of heart and head, Enlightenment critique</keywords>

Now, we have seen that thoughts have their seat in our understanding; and that our moral affections, which ought to be their source, have theirs in our moral sense, or in what we call here the heart.

Thus, Rousseau—whose heart was worth more than his head despite his errors—was able to feel, while concerning himself with languages, the internal fermentation of that radical germ of <margin>God</margin> language which all men carry within themselves. They would recognize it just as he did, if, like him, they had the good fortune that the labors of their heads did not so often veil the riches and the light of their hearts.

Yes, the conviction of the necessity of speech for the institution of speech <note>This refers to the "vicious circle" paradox in 18th-century philosophy: one needs language to agree upon the meaning of words, yet one cannot agree upon anything without language already existing.</note> can only stem from the conviction of the existence of man’s moral sense; and furthermore, whoever recognizes this moral sense must experience impressions of it vivid enough so as not to regard it with indifference, or as a mere opinion fit only to occupy a place in a system.

The first of these objections to arise is the uselessness for them to conceive and pursue such an enterprise: for, from the very start, they must see that regarding the first of these languages, they will have principles against them; and regarding the second, they will have facts against them.

Regarding the first of these languages—the natural and uniform language of the <term>intelligent man: here referring to the human being as a spiritual and rational creature endowed with a soul</term>—they will have principles against them. According to all that we have just seen, one cannot deny that the intelligent man must receive from his source, at birth, the necessary means to express his properties—or a language linked to these very properties. We see that this is also the way nature conducts itself toward the two classes—the animate and the inanimate—which are inferior to the intelligent man. <note>The author suggests that just as animals have innate cries and objects have physical properties, the human soul must have an innate "language" inherent to its nature from the moment of creation.</note>

Once this project was formed, they effectively pruned away all those pressing and imperious foundations <note>The "foundations" refer to the historical and traditional evidence that language was inherited rather than invented from scratch.</note> which—barring a formal design and a deliberately steered way of seeing—are impossible not to recognize as irrevocably existing; and after having pruned them away, what did they put in their place? They have imagined, in their fiction, two private individuals <note>This is a direct reference to the thought experiments of philosophers like Étienne Bonnot de Condillac, who imagined two children isolated in a desert to theorize how they might invent language.</note> deprived, until that time, of all social interaction, even with animals. When, in this state of affairs, I see them, in a way, complaining like you, that it is still a very common opinion today that **language** could not have been **instituted**; when I see them recall, with a kind of commiseration, that only a few years ago the **Philosopher of Geneva** <note>original: "philosophe de Genève." This refers to Jean-Jacques Rousseau (1712–1778), who famously argued in his *Discourse on Inequality* that for language to be established, speech must have already existed—a logical paradox.</note> believed he could demonstrate it without reply, when he wrote that superb proposition concerning which I have just now so formally pronounced myself against you; when I see them, I say, with this tranquil assurance of their triumph, I cannot help but ask them in turn if, to invalidate this famous proposition—that **speech** <term>speech: original "la parole," referring to the faculty of language and the act of speaking.</term> was necessary for the **institution of speech**, a simple sign of disapproval is really sufficient? This is especially true when the deepest and most rigorous observations bring us back under its rule, and when its detractors have nothing to put in its place but obviously truncated foundations, and an order of things that falls apart on its own along with the supposition that gave birth to it.

I nonetheless deeply honor the writers I have in view in this account; I pay the most sincere tribute to their talent, and I beg them not to attribute the form and colors of my plea to any motive of which they might complain.
