Before speaking of another objection that arises against the undertaking of the **institutors of our languages** and the **restorers of our understanding** <term>understanding: original "entendement," a philosophical term referring to the faculty of the mind by which it apprehends ideas.</term>, I ask your permission to present a small **apologue** <note>original: "apologue." A moral fable or allegorical story used to illustrate a point.</note>, perhaps a little childish, but which will only be so because of the context from which it is drawn, and not at all because of the parallels it draws.

In my early childhood, one night, my mother gave me a brother. In the morning, the sight of this unexpected spectacle led me to ask my nanny where this little child had come from; she replied, as most women of that sort do, that he had been picked up in the garden, under a **cabbage leaf**.

Reflecting on the great objects that occupy us and on the manner in which the **philosophers** claim to explain their **origin**, I have sometimes been unable to help thinking, I confess, that, believing us not old enough to understand them, they had decided to thus transport man under the **cabbage leaf**, until we were in a state to know more.

But I also remember that, as small as I was, I did not want to let my nanny have the last word; and that, some time later, I said to her: if it is in the garden and under a cabbage leaf that my brother was found, why is it not also there that one goes to get everything given to him and everything used for him, such as the mother’s milk that nourishes him, the swaddling clothes in which he is wrapped, the fire

<summary>The author uses a childhood story about being told babies come from cabbage patches to satirize philosophers' explanations for the origins of human faculties. He suggests their theories are equally simplistic and fail to account for the complex systems—like nourishment and warmth—that must exist alongside a new being.</summary>

<keywords>origin of language, Enlightenment philosophy, apologue, understanding, childhood anecdote, logic</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the "cabbage leaf" anecdote from page 153 and transitions into a direct philosophical critique of "institutors of language"—likely Enlightenment thinkers like Condillac who argued that language and thought evolved from purely sensory experiences rather than a divine or "radical" source.</meta>

...with which he is warmed, and even the songs with which you seek to cheer him while rocking his cradle? "Be quiet, little boy," she replied to me; "one must not know so much at your age."

What I said then to my nanny, I will say now with even more assurance to the so-called **institutors of our languages** <term>institutors of our languages: "instituteurs de nos langues," referring to philosophers who theorized on how human speech and grammar were first established.</term>; and after having shown them the uselessness of their enterprise, I will show them the falsehood of their teaching.

Indeed, if the supposition upon which they would like to build their edifice is more than risky; if the foundations to which we have tried to bring them back cannot cease to reappear—no matter what care they have taken to discard them—and if they obstinately reclaim their rights, is it not presumable that all the discoveries they make with such sagacity in the pursuit of this non-existent supposition, they would have made equally, and with even more abundance, in the pursuit of that which has all the colors of reality?

Is it not almost certain then that they attribute to the **imaginary source** <note>original: "source imaginaire." The author argues that materialist theories of language origin are mere fantasies.</note> with which they lull themselves, that which belongs to the **radical source** <note>original: "source radicale." ...signs, and of which he is so inclined to pride himself when he makes their discovery?

<margin><unclear>Ideal</unclear></margin> If this is so; if it is true, to return to the style of my **fable** <term>apologue: a moral allegory; here referring to the story of the child found under a cabbage leaf used on the previous page.</term>, that the **institutors of our languages** <note>This refers to thinkers who argued that language was a human invention or "instituted" by social contract rather than being a natural or divine gift.</note>, after having transported man under the cabbage leaf, cannot however find there any of the help he needs; if they are forced despite themselves—and, I even suspect, without their knowing it—to go and take from the paternal home the food with which they wish to nourish him, the magnificent finery with which they wish to adorn him, and the instructions with which they wish to cultivate his intelligence, would it not have been simpler to let him be born in the house, and not give him a supposed mother, while they were receiving everything from his true mother?

...with which he is warmed, and even the songs with which you try to cheer him while rocking his cradle? "Be quiet, little boy," she replied to me; "one should not know so much at your age."

What I said then to my nurse, I will now say with even more confidence to the so-called **institutors of our languages** <note>The author is targeting philosophers who claimed language was a human invention (a social "institution") rather than a divine or innate gift.</note>; and after having shown them the futility of their enterprise, I will show them the falsehood of their teaching.

Indeed, if the supposition upon which they wish to build their edifice is more than risky, if the foundations to which we have tried to bring them back never cease to reappear—no matter what care they have taken to discard them—and if they stubbornly reclaim their rights, is it not likely that all the discoveries they make with such sagacity along the line of this non-existent supposition, they would have made equally, and with even more abundance, along the line that bears all the colors of reality?

Is it not almost certain then that they attribute to the imaginary source they soothe themselves with, what belongs to the **radical source** <note>original: "source radicale," referring to the fundamental root or origin of being.</note> of man’s existence; or to the source of the language he must have received along with existence; to the source of all the wonders that this language must provide for him, since it is the means by which he must manifest all the properties that characterize him; finally, to the source of all the treasures of truths of which he daily offers...

<meta>This page continues the author’s critique of Enlightenment philosophers who believe language was a slow, human-made invention. He argues that such a theory makes nature (or God) appear cruel by denying early humans the essential "right" to communicate.</meta>

<page-num>156</page-num>

...by the teachers of languages <note>original: "instituteurs des langues." The author uses this term to refer to philosophers like Étienne Bonnot de Condillac, who argued that language was a human "institution" or social convention developed over time.</note>, and regardless of the flaws in their teaching, there is another objection to make to them; it is that, if through their systems on the origin of languages they had found the true way in which they were formed, it would be a torment for humanity. For could there be anything more distressing for her, than this enormous length of time she would have had to wait, and this infinite number of centuries she would have had to let pass, before she had discovered, developed, and perfected the means by which she was to satisfy the need she has to converse and to understand one another?

How many generations sacrificed to this discouraging expectation? How many members cut off from the human family, before this human family could have enjoyed its rights? <note>The "rights" mentioned here refer to the natural capacity for speech, which the author believes should be inherent to all humans from the beginning.</note> And when she finally enjoys them, what use will the glory of the species be to that immensity of individuals who would have already passed over the earth—and that, without being able to fulfill the most essential purpose <note>original: "le plus intéressant objet." In the 18th century, "interesting" meant something of vital importance to the soul or being, while "object" referred to one's ultimate aim or end.</note> of their existence?

The lowliest beings in nature are not condemned to this outrageous and inconsistent law. Their language is everywhere beside them, is born with them, and develops progressively, along with all the faculties they have to manifest, as we have seen above. They do not delegate their enjoyments to the future generations of their species. They have received from a benevolent nature the means to procure them for themselves; and if animals, in order to feed themselves, per- <note>The text cuts off mid-word, likely "perfectionnent" (perfect) or "perçoivent" (perceive).</note>

<sig>L 3</sig>

...[per]petuate themselves and communicate together according to their class, had needed to wait for the lessons of their descendants, their species would have been extinct from the first offspring, or rather, it would never have existed.

And you, theorists <note>original: "spéculateurs." The author uses this term to describe Enlightenment philosophers who created abstract systems or "speculations" about the origins of society, often disregarding traditional religious explanations.</note>, you who recognize the superiority of man over all beings; you who, through your own talents, would prove this superiority even if loud doctrines taught the opposite—you would have it that this man, this privileged being, were nevertheless the only one upon whom such a disastrous exception should fall!

You would have it that, while exclusively called to enjoy this superb gift of speech <term>gift of speech: The "don de la parole" refers to the belief that language is an inherent, divine faculty of the human soul, rather than a tool developed through social habit.</term>, which makes man a perpetual marvel, he were nevertheless the only one found condemned to the deprivation of such a beautiful right, until the torrents of centuries, by dint of passing over this precious mine, had managed to uncover all its wealth for him!

You do not fear at all to sacrifice the most sacred rights of man to an ephemeral conception of your mind; and this, throughout an incalculable series of generations!

You do not fear to see your teachings in opposition to the rectitude of a rigorous intelligence!

You do not fear to send your illusions to confront reality!

Oh! You, whom we would like to look upon as the apostles of truth, take care then, for your...

<meta>This page continues the author’s philosophical rebuttal. He moves from discussing the biological impossibility of language as a human invention to a personal defense of his character. He aims to prove that his belief in the "innate" nature of language is based on logic and "moral sense" rather than religious fanaticism or political bias.</meta>

<page-num>( 158 )</page-num>

own glory, as much as for our satisfaction, to take a more consistent and more instructive path; and do not forget that the moral sense, brought back to its true elements, offers you all the means to do so.

Yes, it is this sense, citizen <term>citizen: A term of address standard during the French Revolution, emphasizing legal equality and a break from the titles of the nobility.</term>, which will convince you through your own experience and without miracles, that speech was necessary for the institution of speech <note>original: "la parole a été nécessaire pour l'institution de la parole." This is a central philosophical paradox of the era: for humans to "invent" language, they would have needed language to think and agree upon it in the first place.</note>, because it is a universal and irrevocable law that everything makes its own revelation.

This doctrine must not cause you offense; and if you had studied it deeply, you would not have reproached me, as you did during the session, for having a tendency toward religious ideas. I replied nothing then, because it would have been necessary to speak of myself, and I did not think it appropriate, in such important matters, to transform a question of things into a question of personality. I can return to it now that our discussion is finished, by telling you that in my youth I served for some years as an officer in the regular troops; that being drawn by a taste for study, I returned early to my retirement and my independence; that since then I have been nothing, neither under the Old Regime <term>Old Regime: The "Ancien Régime," referring to the monarchical social and political system in France before the Revolution of 1789.</term> nor under the current regime; and thus, neither my former status nor my present existence offers the slightest varnish of superstition or fanaticism.

I believe I can end my observations here, along with my replies to the variations with which you have enriched the picture of our debate in the assembly. I you...

<meta>This page continues the numbered excerpts from Louis-Claude de Saint-Martin’s "The Man of Desire." The previous page established that language is not merely a human invention but a spiritual fruit; here, the author contrasts this spiritual "Word" with the emptiness of materialist philosophy and the limitations of human artifice compared to divine creation.</meta>

<page-num>162</page-num>

Matter, matter, what a fatal veil you have cast over the truth!

The Word <note>original: "La parole." In Saint-Martin's mysticism, this refers to the divine creative faculty that exists within humans but has been "diminished" or "reduced" by our material existence.</note> has come to the earth only as if through a rebirth; it had first been diminished for us.

It could only be reborn by seed, like vegetation; but it first had to provide its own germ, so that it could afterwards produce its fruits among the human species.

(No. 85.) The materialist doctrine has nothing lively or sensible to support itself; it is reduced to crying out everywhere: Nothingness, nothingness; and it cannot strike any active blow against its adversaries.

It is even vulnerable from all sides and has not the slightest defensive force to offer.

It resembles those gods of stone and wood who, according to <term>Baruch|A reference to the "Letter of Jeremiah" found in the Book of Baruch in the biblical Apocrypha, which satirizes the helplessness of man-made idols.</term>, could defend themselves neither from the insults of the air nor the filth of insects; who could neither walk nor even support themselves on their feet without being attached with iron clamps, like criminals.

(No. 163.) When will you open your eyes to these childish wonders that come from your hands?

You can only perform transpositions <note>Saint-Martin argues that human "creation" is merely moving existing matter around, whereas true creation is a spiritual act.</note>, whereas the works of nature are continual creations. Everywhere she follows her work, and never does she take notice of yours.

(No. 263.) Man, you are such a great being that only your action alone can demonstrate your sublimity to you!

<summary>Saint-Martin critiques materialist philosophy as a hollow "nothingness" and compares human artifice to helpless idols, contrasting them with the living, creative power of the spiritual Word and Nature.</summary>

<margin>to operate, not to create; author <note>Saint-Martin distinguishes between human "operation"—acting upon existing things—and divine "creation" from nothing.</note></margin>
You can find no space between doubt and miracles. If you do not operate worlds yourself, you cease to believe that you were born of the Author of worlds.

*The New Man.* <note>Original: "Le Nouvel Homme," a work by Saint-Martin published in 1792 emphasizing the need for inward spiritual rebirth.</note> (No. 22.) Such is this inexhaustible mine, in which there are no riches we cannot find, but which has become as if foreign even to its owner; because men, greedy for external sciences, have directed all the faculties of their spirit outward, instead of directing them toward that interior which would have taught them everything, and would have lavished all its treasures upon them.

Thus they have allowed this mine to be closed by the debris that falls there daily; and it has become so covered that they no longer believe in its existence, and have subsequently made every effort to prevent us from believing in it in our turn, and from seeking to work it.

(No. 70.) It is in this respect that you must consider yourself a sort of <term>universality|The concept that man is a "microcosm" or a small-scale map of the entire universe, containing divine, spiritual, and natural elements within himself.</term>, since you find within yourself the divine world, the spiritual world, and the natural world, and because by this you are the image of him who produced everything, and who is everything; but you are only the image of him who produced everything, and who is everything, insofar as you remain in him, and as he himself gives form to all your faculties and all your substances: for how would you be a partial universality, if you were not continually conceived, engendered, and nourished by the great universality?

<margin>let us speak</margin>
*Ecce Homo* <note>Latin: "Behold the Man." This refers to Saint-Martin's 1792 treatise which warns against the dangers of seeking spiritual phenomena (like those in "Theosophy" or "Illuminism") without first undergoing moral purification.</note>, (No. 1.) Whether or not we make use of the treasures of truth that this divine contract <note>The "divine contract" refers to the inherent spiritual bond or covenant between the Creator and humanity.</note> [bestows]...

<summary>Saint-Martin describes the human soul as an "inexhaustible mine" of divine knowledge that has been buried under the "debris" of external, material concerns. He argues that man is a microcosm containing all levels of reality—divine, spiritual, and natural—as long as he remains connected to the Creator.</summary>

<keywords>The New Man, Ecce Homo, internal mine, universality, microcosm, divine contract, spiritual faculties, inwardness</keywords>

--- Page 110 ---
<meta>This page continues the excerpts from Saint-Martin's "The New Man." The previous page concluded with the idea of man as a "universality" or microcosm containing the divine, spiritual, and natural worlds. This page explores why the loss of spiritual awareness doesn't destroy our innate divine connection and contrasts the depth of spiritual truth with the "surface" truths of physical science.</meta>

<page-num>( 164 )</page-num>

...reveals, it is undoubtedly something that must have the greatest influence on our true satisfactions, but it has none on the existence of these same treasures, nor on that of that portion of ourselves which happens to be their receptacle. Thus, the deprivation of this sublime feeling in withered souls <note>Original: "ames altérées." In this context, it refers to souls that are parched, corrupted, or have lost their original spiritual thirst.</note>, and all the irrationalities <note>Original: "déraisonnemens."</note> resulting from it, can annihilate neither the necessary and eternal principle of beings, nor the divine analogy <note>The mystical concept that the structure of the human soul mirrors the structure of the Divine.</note> that we all have with him.

(N.° 2.) Man may no longer be what he was, but he can always feel what he ought to be. He can always feel the inferiority of his perishable and material substance, which has only a passive power over him—that of absorbing his faculties through the disorders and opacity of which it is susceptible; while his **thinking being** has the active power to create, so to speak, a thousand faculties in his corporeal being, which it would not have had by nature and without the will of man. This is a difference we present here by design to the "man of matter" <note>A term for someone focused entirely on the physical world, ignoring the spiritual.</note>, and it is too striking for him to be excusable for not perceiving there some vestiges of his ancient dignity and the supremacy of his thought; a difference, I say, which could raise him higher, and prove to him how right they were to say that **interior truths** must be much more certain and instructive than **geometrical truths**, because the latter rest only on surfaces, whereas the former are born actively from the very center, and allow a glimpse of its depth.

<summary>Saint-Martin argues that even when humans lose their spiritual awareness, their underlying divine connection remains intact. He distinguishes between the passive, "opaque" nature of the physical body and the active, creative power of the soul, which accesses truths far deeper than those of surface-level mathematics or science.</summary>

<keywords>divine analogy, thinking being, interior truths, materialism, spiritual rebirth, Saint-Martin, human dignity, microcosm</keywords>

They will all be printed in the session transcripts. It is good for the nation to learn, from this moment on, how many students the Normal Schools contain who are worthy of soon teaching human knowledge in those Central Schools <note>Central Schools: Secondary schools established by the revolutionary government to replace the old clerical colleges, emphasizing science and reason.</note> that the Convention <note>The National Convention: The sovereign ruling body of France during this period of the Revolution.</note> has just decreed.

# COURSE

## ON THE ANALYSIS

### OF

### THE HUMAN UNDERSTANDING. <note>This subject, based on the works of philosophers like Locke and Condillac, sought to understand how humans acquire knowledge through senses and language.</note>

->(9 *Ventôse, Year Three.*) <insert>1795.</insert><- <note>Ventôse was the sixth month of the French Republican Calendar, occurring in late winter. 9 Ventôse, Year III, corresponds to February 27, 1795.</note>

->BY CITIZEN GARAT.<- <note>Dominique-Joseph Garat (1749–1833) was a philosopher, journalist, and politician who served as Minister of Justice during the Revolution.</note>

Since the last session, many letters have been written to me by the Students of the Normal Schools. These letters, and I exclude none of them, could only have been written by very well-educated men; and, what is worth

...you will find that the floor belongs to him by right: I invite him to rise: it is Citizen Saint-Martin. <note>Louis-Claude de Saint-Martin (1743–1803), known as "The Unknown Philosopher," was a French theosophist and mystic. ### CITIZEN PROFESSOR,

When the session was interrupted, I was saying that you had fully satisfied me in the conference of 19 Pluviôse <note>19 Pluviôse, Year III, corresponds to February 7, 1795. Pluviôse was the "rainy month" in the French Republican Calendar.</note> by reducing, according to my question, all those expressions of *creating our ideas* and *recreating our understanding* <note>Saint-Martin is questioning the Enlightenment idea that humans "create" their own minds through sensory experience alone.</note>; by reducing them, I say, to their true value; by recognizing that if nature has not begun by sowing the gift of talent and genius in a man, culture—so necessary to those who might be favored with these gifts—could never create them within him. Consequently, this culture, which at first seemed destined to occupy such a major rank, nevertheless falls back into the class of subsidiary causes and gives way to those profound foundations which we may call *Primordial Causes* <note>A central theme in Saint-Martin's philosophy: the belief that human intelligence has a divine or innate origin that precedes education.</note>. These, however difficult they may be to obtain, are nonetheless placed among the data that must serve us in explaining the diversities of the human spirit. You also satisfied me regarding the *internal organs*, by informing me that your doctrine on this point was limited to the care of working to perfect them; though I must frankly confess to you that, under this expression *internal organs*, my idea went further than the cerebellum <note>The "cervelet" or cerebellum. Garat likely argued for a biological, material basis for the mind, while Saint-Martin implies a more spiritual or "inner" faculty.</note>: emboldened by these first amendments, I will venture to propose three others to you. Here is the subject of the first.

<meta>This page continues the introduction by Dominique-Joseph Garat from page 6. The final word on the previous page began with the syllable "pen-", which is completed here by "serez" to form the French word *penserez* (you will think/realize).</meta>

...will realize that the floor rightly belongs to him:
I invite him to stand: it is Citizen Saint-Martin <note>Louis-Claude de Saint-Martin (1743–1803): Known as "The Unknown Philosopher," he was a prominent mystical philosopher who often disagreed with the materialist views of the Enlightenment professors at the École Normale.</note>.

->*Citizen Saint-Martin.*<-

**CITIZEN PROFESSOR**,

When the session was interrupted, I was saying that you had fully satisfied me in the conference of 19 Pluviôse <note>19 Pluviôse: A date in the French Republican Calendar, corresponding to February 7, 1795. This was during the first year of the École Normale's operation.</note>, by reducing—at my request—all those expressions of *creating our ideas* and *recreating our understanding*; by reducing them, I say, to their true value. You recognized that if nature has not first sown the gifts of talent and genius in a man, then culture <term>culture: In the 18th-century sense, this refers to "cultivation" or education—the process of refining the mind through study and training.</term>—however necessary it may be for those favored with these gifts—could never create them within him. Consequently, this education, which initially seemed to occupy such a dominant position, belongs instead to the category of subsidiary causes. It gives way to those profound foundations that we may call *Primordial Causes*; and these, however difficult they may be to obtain, must nevertheless be placed among the facts that should help us explain the diversity of the human mind. You also satisfied me regarding *internal organs*, by informing me that your doctrine on this point was limited to the effort of working to perfect them; though I must frankly confess to you that, under this expression of *internal organs*, my concept went much further than the cerebellum <note>The "Cerebellum" (cervelet) refers here to the physical brain. Saint-Martin is politely hinting that he believes human thought involves spiritual or metaphysical "organs" rather than just physical biological structures.</note>. Emboldened by these first amendments, I shall venture to propose three others to you. Here is the subject of the first.

<meta>This page continues a dialogue between the philosopher Louis-Claude de Saint-Martin (known as "The Unknown Philosopher") and an unnamed Professor. *De St.-Martin.* I am convinced of it.

*The Professor.* However, when I receive, in the presence of the sun, the sensations given to me by that brilliant star, which warms and illuminates the earth; do I know anything of it other than the sensations themselves that I receive from it?

*De St.-Martin.* You feel the sensations; but the reflections you will make upon the existence of the sun...

*The Professor.* Wait; we will get to reflections. First of all, let us stop for a moment on the sensations themselves. I say that the sensations I receive give me, at once, the *impression* and the *knowledge* of light and heat; and I add that knowledge here is no different from the *impression*.

*De St.-Martin.* I grant that they give you the sensation; but it is your <term>understanding<note>French: *entendement*. In 18th-century philosophy, this refers to the faculty of the mind that processes and organizes sensory data into coherent thoughts.</note></term> that gives you knowledge of it.

*The Professor.* But, pray tell, in what way could the sensations I receive from a thing, and the knowledge I might acquire of it, differ, since I can only know of that thing the sensations I receive from it? To know a thing is merely to clearly disentangle the sensations it gives you; to clearly disentangle them is merely to feel them distinctly; to feel them distinctly is merely to feel them.

<meta>Page 28 continues the author's comparative analysis of the origins of human knowledge, moving from the theories of Plato and Descartes to the more radical theological philosophy of Nicolas Malebranche.</meta>

<margin>?</margin>
It is in <term>Malebranche<note>Nicolas Malebranche (1638–1715) was a French Oratorian priest and philosopher who sought to synthesize the thought of St. Augustine and Descartes, leading to his famous doctrine that we "see all things in God."</note></term>, in fact, that everything which had begun to be *eternal* and *divine* in <term>Plato<note>The ancient Greek philosopher who argued that the physical world is a mere shadow of a higher, eternal reality of "Forms" or "Ideas."</note></term>, fully achieved that status. It is in Malebranche that everything in man is thought by God—that everything is God.

Let us try to bring some precision to this language; it does not seem very susceptible to it.

Malebranche is even less inclined than Plato and <term>Descartes<note>René Descartes (1596–1650), often called the father of modern philosophy, who emphasized reason and the existence of "innate ideas" present in the mind from birth.</note></term> to recognize our sensations as the source of our ideas: where, then, do we draw them from in his view? Is it, as Plato would have it, from *eternal designs*, of which the beings contained in the universe are the copies and not the models? No. Is it from ideas prior to our existence on earth, in *innate ideas*, as Descartes claims? No. According to Malebranche, the existence of spiritual souls and their relationships with the universe are not yet sufficient to explain the formation of our ideas. While he agrees with Plato in denying us the faculty of composing our ideas from our sensations, he does not agree with Descartes that upon arriving in the world we find them all engraved in advance within our souls. It is from God that we draw them, according to Malebranche; every true idea we acquire is a discovery we make in God: it is God himself who produces our ideas within us; or rather, our ideas themselves are like portions of the divinity.

Such a doctrine is bound to surprise; it has

...even enough to terrify both philosophers and theologians. Well now! Malebranche, who was both a theologian and a philosopher at once, far from being terrified by it, grew bolder in developing it: he did not stop; he went further.

After having maintained that no *idea* can be produced by the faculties given to man alone, nor by the sole relationships established between man and nature, Malebranche maintains that all motions are the actual acts of God, just like all ideas; he maintains that without the positive and continual intervention of God, all motion, as well as every idea, is impossible; and if one cries out to Malebranche, *what are you saying? What of the idea the murderer has when he sharpens a dagger, and the motions of the assassin when he plunges that dagger home?...* Malebranche, whose soul was so pure and religious, shudders... but his doctrine does not retreat: and his mind is so prodigiously fertile in subtle and ineffable <note>Incapable of being expressed in words; often used here to describe Malebranche's complex metaphysical justifications.</note> explanations, that his mind is fully reassured against absurdities, and his faith fully against impieties.

<meta>This page continues the author's argument regarding the origin of human knowledge. ...often philosophers: ask the plowman who cultivates our fields how he acquired the exact and complete ideas he has of his **plow**; he will answer you, without hesitating, that he acquired them by seeing his plow, by touching it, by steering it, by repairing it, and by taking it apart as needed.

A **geometer** does not acquire the ideas of a triangle and a rectangle any differently; nor does a **metaphysician** acquire those metaphysical ideas, which only elude the senses when one persists in seeking them outside of sensations, and consequently, outside the boundaries of reason: a metaphysician and a geometer would therefore give you nearly the same answer as the plowman, if they knew as well as he does where they draw their ideas from; but that is what they rarely know. Since their ideas are more numerous, more complicated, and more lengthily and confusedly deduced from the sensations where they take their origin, they are unskillful at tracing back to this source and following it through all its derivations. This is something one can only learn well from an excellent <term>metaphysics<note>In this context, metaphysics refers to the study of the nature of human knowledge and the mind, rather than the study of the supernatural.</note>; and the metaphysics which is not always good, even when it seeks the elements and materials of our ideas in sensations, is always detestable when it finds them elsewhere. Most scholars, in the midst of their ideas and their sciences—so poorly constructed, and whose design and artifice they profoundly ignore—resemble the modern **Egyptians**, the <term>Copts<note>original: "Cophtes." The author refers to the native Christian population of Egypt, who in the 18th century were often depicted by European travelers as living in the shadow of ancient monuments (the pyramids) whose origins and construction they no longer understood.</note></term> placed beside the **pyramids**. <meta>This page continues the author's defense of Sensationalist philosophy. Having established on the previous page that all ideas—even abstract ones—originate in physical sensations, the author now specifically addresses morality. He refutes the then-popular theory of a "moral sense" (an innate biological faculty for discerning right and wrong) in favor of a model where morality is a complex product of all the senses processed by reason.</meta>

...allow me to rectify some of those ideas that you attribute to me.

I could not have said anywhere, as you suppose, that independently of the other senses, man has a **universal sense** for judging the **morality** of actions. I would be a bit ashamed and confused, I admit, if such a vague idea, such an un-analytical word, had escaped me while professing <term>analysis<note>In Enlightenment philosophy, "analysis" refers to the method of deconstructing complex thoughts into the simple sensory impressions that formed them.</note></term>. If there were a **universal sense**, it would not be exclusively **moral**: if there were a **universal sense**, no other sense would be necessary for us; and we would have no others.

I know nothing **universal**, strictly speaking, but the **universe**.

You have not only misunderstood, but misheard my words. The sound and the meaning of those I may have used present different ideas.

In rejecting the opinion of an organ, of a particular sense exclusively intended and appropriated for the formation of our **moral ideas**, I said that **moral sentiment** did not arise from the impression of only one of our senses alone, but from the impression of all our senses at once. It is precisely thus that **reason** itself is born and formed; and according to me, true **morality** is what is most perfect and most necessary among the works of **reason**.

You think otherwise: you do not want the faculty that judges what **IS** *good* to be the same...

When I want to fix my thought and yours upon this infinite number of different means that man can use to display his ideas before others and before himself, I use the word <term>signs</term>; it applies equally to all these means of whatever nature or kind they may be, or can be.

When I wish to speak to you more particularly of those signs which consist of sounds emitted by the voice and struck by the tongue, I use in turn, but not indifferently, sometimes the word <term>speech</term>, sometimes the word <term>language</term>.

immense scope, including the clucking sounds of the <term>Troglodytes</term> <note>Literally "cave-dwellers," a term used in 18th-century philosophy to refer to prehistoric or legendary primitive tribes.</note> and the <term>Hottentots</term> <note>original: "Hottentots." This is a colonial-era, now-derogatory term for the Khoekhoe people of Southern Africa; the author uses it here to exemplify what he perceived as "primitive" vocalizations.</note>, the picturesque and harmonious expressions of the languages of Homer and Virgil, the ibis and <note>original: "phallum"</note> phalluses carved on the obelisks of Egypt, the digits of the arithmetician, the bars of the algebraist, the knotted cords of the <term>Peruvians</term> <note>A reference to the "quipu," a sophisticated system of knotted strings used by the Inca Empire to record data and narratives.</note>, the full images of the Mexicans <note>Referring to the pictorial codices of the indigenous peoples of Mexico.</note>, and the reduced images of the Chinese <note>The author characterizes Chinese characters as "reduced images," or ideograms that have evolved from more literal pictures.</note>.

Notice, however, that the meaning of the word *signs*, far from becoming confused within this vast scope it embraces, acquires in many respects a more philosophical precision. One sees or feels better in this word *signs* than in the words *speech* and *languages* that all languages and all signs are but representations of our ideas; a very simple truth, but one very often forgotten and always ready to escape us.

Notice again that these words *signs* and *languages*, which have, in a sense, their own distinct and well-defined departments, can sometimes be moved from one department to the other; in this transfer, they do not only acquire more elegance along with more novelty; they fulfill their logical and metaphysical functions better: they make the mind more attentive, which they surprise a little; and when one is very attentive, one is very close to being a philosopher. The first who said that all signs are languages, and that all languages are signs, were demonstrating both wit and good metaphysics.

Regarding Rousseau's opinion, he fell into it through an oversight; and his oversight stems from his system against society. 

Rousseau wanted to prove that society is an institution against nature; and since speech is the great instrument of social institutions, he was led to believe that speech, in its creation, was something beyond the natural powers of man.

But do you know why languages appeared to him to be beyond the powers of the savage man? It is because instead of reasoning based on the languages of savage tribes, he reasoned based on the languages of civilized peoples. Certainly, the language of <term>Cicero</term> <note>Marcus Tullius Cicero (106–43 BCE), the Roman orator celebrated for his mastery of Latin eloquence.</note> and the language of <term>Fénélon</term> <note>François Fénélon (1651–1715), a French archbishop and writer renowned for his elegant and harmonious literary style.</note> could never have been created by <term>Hottentots</term> <note>original: "Hottentots." It is very true that the establishment of speech is indispensable for establishing speech as it existed in Athens under <term>Pericles</term> <note>The "Golden Age" of Athens (5th century BCE), a period of great intellectual and rhetorical achievement.</note>; in Paris, during the century of Louis XIV; in London, in the mouths of <term>Pope</term> and <term>Addison</term> <note>Alexander Pope (1688–1744) and Joseph Addison (1672–1719), leading English writers of the Enlightenment known for their refined prose and poetry.</note>: thus, the speech of these centuries and of these learned men was established by the conventions and by the speech of many men and many centuries already very enlightened. <meta>This page continues the author's argument against Jean-Jacques Rousseau's theory on the origin of language. <page-num>( 41 )</page-num>

...no convention established by speech, no prior knowledge is needed to make <term>savages</term> <note>original: "sauvages." In 18th-century philosophy, this term referred to "natural man" or indigenous peoples living outside European-style civilization, often used as a theoretical starting point for human history.</note> let out inarticulate cries, or even the articulated sounds, which are the products of their sensations and the mechanical movements of their organs; and as these sensations and these movements are the same in all those who utter these cries and sounds, and in all those who hear them, everyone uses them, and everyone understands them equally, without prior agreement, without earlier convention. For all this, no other school is needed than the forests.

This is not all: before articulated sounds, there are gestures; and before gestures made by design, there are involuntary gestures—that is to say, all the movements that the needs and the passions of savages imprint upon every part of their bodies.

This is the beginning of languages, of speech: this beginning is not the work of man, it is that of nature. It supposes nothing prior in the history of the human species; it is sufficient to explain everything that follows.

<term>Rousseau</term> <note>Jean-Jacques Rousseau (1712–1778), whose "Discourse on the Origin and Basis of Inequality Among Men" argued that the gap between animal cries and human language was so vast it required a "miracle" or divine intervention to bridge.</note> wanted to discover the sources of a great river, and he looked for them at its mouth: this was not the way to find them; but it was the way to believe—as it was once believed of the **sources of the Nile** <note>A popular 18th-century metaphor. I have been even more circumspect than Locke; I have neither stated nor announced any opinion on the relationship between matter and thought: however, it has been said and printed several times in journals and pamphlets that I was making matter an eternal substance, and thought one of its attributes. <meta>This page continues the professor’s defense of his philosophical method. Here, he distinguishes his empirical approach—based on the "testimony of the senses"—from the speculative claims of both materialists and spiritualists.</meta>

<page-num>44</page-num>

opposite opinions would obtain from me. <term>Religious dogmas</term> <note>Strictly defined doctrines or beliefs presented as authoritative truth by a church.</note>, whatever they may be, can never rely on anything but <term>historical proofs</term> <note>Evidence based on testimony and records of the past, which the author considers less certain than direct observation.</note>: and these proofs, at their greatest strength, can never outweigh the force of either a mathematical demonstration or even a truth of <term>natural history</term> <note>In the 18th century, "natural history" referred to the systematic study of the physical world, including biology and geology.</note> demonstrated to the human mind by the *invariable and universal testimony of our senses.*

If I saw clearly, then, that our sensations are the phenomena of a certain organization of matter, just as I see clearly that all our thoughts are nothing but *simple, abstract, or combined* sensations, I would base my doctrine on the first of these principles as I do on the second. But the second is a fact for me that I adopt; and the first is a hypothesis of the <term>materialists</term> <note>Philosophers who argue that everything in the universe, including thought and consciousness, is the result of material interactions.</note> with which I do not concern myself, because if they have no demonstration to present to me, I have no demonstration to offer them either.

The <term>spiritualist</term> <note>Those who believe in the existence of an immaterial soul or spirit as distinct from the body.</note> and the materialist both say more than I do; they do not know any more. I renounce climbing as high as they do, but in order to rely on a foundation within my reach, and which nothing can shake.

What makes a theory of the <term>human understanding</term> <note>A common philosophical term of the era, popularized by Locke, referring to the study of how the mind acquires knowledge.</note>, based on sensations, so frightening for the spiritualists, is that, being fully persuaded that matter cannot think, even if God willed it, they nonetheless believe they conceive how the

<meta>This page continues the author’s critique of how both spiritualists and materialists handle the relationship between the body and the soul. matter can feel, they believe they see that sensations belong to matter. What an illusion! And how dangerous it is for their dogmas! But the true <term>métaphysicien</term> <note>A philosopher who investigates the fundamental nature of reality, including the relationship between mind and matter.</note> takes pity on both the triumph of the one and the foolish imprudence of the other: the metaphysician has observed well that certain movements in certain of our <term>organes</term> <note>The biological structures of the body, such as the eyes, ears, or nerves.</note> always precede our <term>sensations</term>; but he does not better conceive how the <term>mouvement</term> of an organ produces a sensation. The ideas attached to these three words—*<term>organ</term>*, *<term>movement</term>*, *<term>sensation</term>*—are never linked closely enough in true <term>métaphysique</term> as to not leave certain gaps, in a manner of speaking, in which <term>spiritualisme</term> can happily place its hypothesis, if it is philosophical, or its dogmas, if it is religious. The <term>métaphysique</term> that does not profess spiritualism is not, therefore, its enemy: it rather comes to its aid.

This is so true that <term>Condillac</term> <note>Étienne Bonnot de Condillac (1714–1780), an influential French philosopher who argued that all human knowledge and faculties come from the senses.</note> and <term>Bonnet</term> <note>Charles Bonnet (1720–1793), a Swiss naturalist and philosopher who sought to bridge the gap between biology and the soul.</note> count among their most rigorous demonstrations the impossibility of conceiving sensations within extension—that is to say, within matter <note>The author refers to the Cartesian idea that matter's primary quality is "extension" (occupying space), whereas thought does not occupy space.</note>; and what is even more remarkable is that <term>Bonnet</term>, who is eminently religious, always seeks the physical causes of our sensations in a sort of anatomy of the <term>cerveau</term> <note>Brain.</note> and the <term>système nerveux</term> <note>Nervous system.</note>, in the hidden movements of fibers and fibrils; while the Abbé de...

...opposing views would obtain from me. <term>Religious dogmas</term> <note>The core established beliefs of a religion.</note>, whatever they may be, can never rely on anything but <term>historical proofs</term>: and these proofs, even at their greatest strength, can never outweigh the force of either a <term>mathematical demonstration</term> or even a truth of <term>natural history</term> demonstrated to the human mind by the invariable and universal testimony of our senses.

If I saw with evidence, then, that our <term>sensations</term> <note>The basic experiences of the world through the five senses.</note> are phenomena of a certain organization of <term>matter</term>, just as I see with evidence that all our thoughts are only simple, abstract, or combined sensations, I would found my doctrine on the first of these principles as I do on the second. But the second is a fact for me that I adopt; and the first is a hypothesis of the <term>materialists</term> <note>Philosophers who believe that only physical matter exists and that thought is just a physical process.</note> with which I do not concern myself, because if they have no demonstration to present to me, I have no demonstration to offer them either.

Both the <term>spiritualist</term> <note>In this context, a philosopher who believes in an immaterial soul distinct from the body.</note> and the materialist say more than I do; they do not know any more. I refuse to climb as high as they do, instead choosing to rest upon a base within my reach, and which nothing can shake.

What makes a theory of the <term>human understanding</term> <note>The mental capacity to comprehend, reason, and form ideas.</note>, founded on sensations, so frightening for the spiritualists, is that—while firmly persuaded that matter cannot think, even if God willed it—they nevertheless believe they conceive how...

<summary>The author argues that religious claims based on historical evidence cannot supersede mathematical or sensory truths. He proposes a philosophy based on the observable fact that thoughts come from sensations, while remaining neutral on the unprovable debate between materialists and spiritualists regarding the nature of the soul.</summary>

The ideas attached to these three words *organ*, *move-*
*ment*, *sensation* never link closely enough in true metaphysics not to leave intervals, so to speak, in which spiritualism can fortunately place its hypothesis, if it is philosophical, or its dogmas, if it is religious. Metaphysics, which does not profess spiritualism, is therefore not its enemy: it comes rather to its aid.

<term>Condillac</term> <note>Étienne Bonnot de Condillac (1714–1780), a prominent French Enlightenment philosopher who argued that all human knowledge and faculties derive from sensations.</note>, who speaks little of religion, almost always sets aside these material facts associated with the causes and acts of intelligence. In this, I have imitated and will always imitate Condillac; not for fear of appearing to be a <term>materialist</term> <note>A philosopher who believes that everything in the universe, including thought and consciousness, is the result of physical matter and its movements.</note>; if I were one, I would fear little appearing so; not for fear of becoming one in these physiological researches; if materialism were a truth, I would believe it useful like all truths; but because all this mechanism of our nerves, of their system and their relations with the brain and sensations, is still much too poorly known. It is natural to believe that it is there that light begins: but it is common to find there only uncertain, obscure facts, only darkness; and one must only begin to walk where one sees the light dawn; that is at <term>sensations</term> <note>The basic experiences of the world gathered through the senses.</note>.

Sensations! These are thus the first phenomena that sound <term>metaphysics</term> <note>The branch of philosophy that deals with the first principles of things, including abstract concepts such as being, knowing, and the nature of the mind.</note> seizes upon; and with them, it explains all the phenomena of the human spirit, with as much clarity and with as much certainty, at least, as experimental physics and chemistry explain some phenomena of nature.

The <term>spirit</term> <note>Or "mind" (French: *l'esprit*). In this context, it refers to the non-material seat of intelligence and self-improvement.</note>, he said, cultivates itself, it perfects itself; and matter can neither perfect itself, nor cultivate itself: therefore, it cannot have the attributes of the spirit; therefore it cannot be the being that thinks.

This argument cannot provide any conviction; for it provides no enlightenment.

This assertion: *matter cannot cultivate and perfect itself* is the same as this assertion: *matter cannot feel*; for if it could feel, it could have ideas; through ideas it could work upon itself, cultivate itself, perfect itself, and cultivate and perfect all that is not organized to feel. It is therefore the question itself, posed in other terms, that Citizen St.-Martin offers as his solution.

The best proofs that matter cannot think are drawn from our inability to conceive that <term>extension</term> <note>In Cartesian philosophy, the defining property of matter is "extension" (taking up space), which was seen as fundamentally incompatible with the nature of thought.</note> and thought belong to the same <term>substance</term>, to matter. **Duhamel.** Citizen, I still read **Condillac** <note>Étienne Bonnot de Condillac (1714–1780), a prominent French Enlightenment philosopher who argued that all human knowledge and mental faculties derive entirely from sensory experience.</note>, I always read him with interest. But I do not except it; on the contrary, I declare that the plan of this work is not <term>analytical</term> <note>The "analytical method" involves breaking complex ideas down into their simplest sensory components. **Condillac** grants memory, judgment, desires, love, hatred, fear, and all the passions to a statue limited to the sense of smell: but, if we attach ideas to these terms, a statue limited to the sense of smell has only sensations; and sensations are nothing other than movements excited in the <term>nervous system</term> <note>original: "genre nerveux." At this time, philosophers were beginning to link mental activity directly to the physiological vibrations or movements of nerves.</note> by comparison with external objects. The statue that experiences only these movements has only its nerves in motion, and has no means to stop or fix these movements: one sensation destroys another sensation; and, by the last one, it judges no more than it did at the first.

**Memory** presupposes that one attaches ideas or sensations to <term>signs</term> <note>Words or symbols. Enlightenment thinkers argued that without language or "signs," we could not "anchor" a thought in our minds to remember it later.</note> which hold them within the faculties; or it presupposes <term>sensibility</term>. **Reminiscence** <note>The act of recalling a past sensation to current consciousness.</note>, which is a necessary faculty for judging, presupposes...

...likewise, desire is a state of our faculties directed toward an object; desire presupposes the knowledge of an object. Will it be said that sensation is the knowledge of an object? This is an abstract and inconceivable idea: an object and its sensation are one and the same. Love, hatred, and all the passions presuppose many acquired ideas.

Now, if by the "analytical method" <note>The analytical method was a hallmark of Enlightenment science, popularized by figures like Locke and Condillac; it involved breaking down complex ideas into their simplest sensory components.</note> one means an order of ideas analogous to their composition and their generalization in order to arrive at a total idea, you will see, no doubt, that the method followed by **Condillac** is hardly analytical. Furthermore, it seems to me a false view to wish to form the mind by taking, among the sensations, those which are received by each of our senses; and especially if one begins with one of the senses <note>Referring to the sense of smell, which Condillac used as the starting point for his statue because he considered it the sense that contributes the least to the human mind.</note> that provides neither form nor color; it is only after having acquired real ideas that one can possess intellectual faculties.

->To summarize.<-

I ask: 1st. Can the sensations of smell be fixed? If they can be neither fixed nor linked, can they be compared?

2nd. If they cannot be compared, can one who possesses nothing but sensations have the judgment of all the intellectual faculties? Within the variety of sensations that contain these faculties, and which are developed to arrive at the total idea of the understanding, can one truly see an analytical order there?

**The Professor.** I regret that Condillac is not here to answer you; he would respond, I believe, to all your difficulties; and he could not answer you without adding to our enlightenment <note>The original term "lumières" refers to the intellectual progress of the 18th-century Enlightenment, a period defined by the application of reason to all areas of human life.</note>.

Citizen <note>A standard form of address during the French Revolutionary period, reflecting the era's egalitarian ideals.</note>, I know with what dexterity you wield this instrument of analysis; however, I must tell you that here you do not seem to make an equally successful use of it.

My objective is not to defend, without restriction, the plan of Condillac’s work on sensations. You know that I myself have taken a different path.

However, let us place ourselves from the point of view that Condillac adopted in his *Treatise on Sensations*; let us suppose a statue organized like a man; it has not yet received life, but it is capable of receiving it. Let us further suppose that we grant life and the exercise not to all of this statue's senses at once, but to a single one of its senses first, and then to all of them successively. In this hypothesis, I can very well conceive how, with the exercise of a single sense—even the sense most limited in its acquisitions, that of smell—the statue can receive pleasure and pain; and as soon as this statue *enjoys* and *suffers* in turn, I conceive how it rises to those modifications of sensibility <term>sensibility: the capacity of an organism to respond to sensory stimuli, which Enlightenment thinkers believed was the root of all human thought.</term> that we call *attention*, *memory*, *imagination*, *desire*, *will*, etc., etc., etc.

Let us follow for a moment the workings of Condillac's mind in this *Treatise on Sensations* <note>Published in 1754, Étienne Bonnot de Condillac’s *Traité des sensations* argued that all human knowledge and faculties derive solely from sensory experience.</note>; I repeat it; many things are missing from this work; but it will always be poorly appreciated if it is not considered one of the most useful creations of the human spirit.

Condillac presents flowers to the sense of smell of his statue: some have perfumes that please it, others have odors that offend it. After having given sensations to the statue, they are taken away: by turns, we bring it out of nothingness <note>In this context, "nothingness" refers to the statue’s initial state of total lack of consciousness or memory before receiving a sensory stimulus.</note> and make it return there. But neither the vivid and pleasant sensations, nor the vivid and painful sensations, return forever to nothingness. After the flowers have been removed, the organ of smell, which has received movements and sensations, has also received a disposition to repeat these same movements in the absence of the flowers—to reproduce these same sensations. It repeats them, then, or rather they repeat themselves, and the same sensations reappear; they are the same, although they are weakened.

->There already is memory.<-

Condillac’s final works appear more analytical to you than his first; I believe they all are quite analytical: but it is certain that in the last ones, his <term>analysis</term> <note>For Condillac, "analysis" was the primary tool of philosophy, involving the breaking down of complex ideas into the basic sensations that originally formed them.</note> is much more perfect. This fact allows for an observation that shows us how much sound <term>metaphysics</term> <note>In the Enlightenment context, metaphysics referred to the study of the origin of human ideas and the nature of the mind.</note> is useful to the mind; how well-suited it is, not only to constantly add to one's enlightenment, but to perfect the very instrument with which one can acquire it. From his first writing on metaphysics, which was exempt neither from errors nor defects, Condillac nevertheless proves himself the best metaphysician of the century. The processes of his mind were more reliable than the processes of the minds of our most famous philosophers; but whatever subject he treated in this first work, and in those following, all essentially had the objective of exploring the faculties by which man thinks; and for their aim, the perfection of analysis and method: thus his analysis becomes ever more precise, and his works ever more illuminating. The latest is always the best, though I do not say the most important. Nearly all minds have their brightest spark in youth, their maturity in middle age, and they decline toward the twilight of years and life: but Condillac's mind constantly acquires more certainty, more clarity, and more breadth: it grows stronger in old age more

*Duhamel.* I said, citizen <note>The term "citoyen" marks this text as a product of the French Revolutionary era, where traditional titles were replaced with egalitarian ones.</note>, that in the state of our faculties, in order to have a desire, it was necessary to have knowledge of the object.

However, all the other senses also give us sensations that we desire: at its mother’s breast, the child desires the milk that nourishes it, even before knowing either the milk or the mother’s breast. . . . But let us conclude this lecture, which has already gone on so long; these questions belong to other parts of this course.

## Louis-Claude de Saint-Martin, to citizen Garat, professor of human understanding at the Normal Schools <note>The Écoles Normales were established in 1794 to train teachers in a standardized, Republican curriculum based on Enlightenment principles.</note>.

->CITIZEN,<-

You had the right to review the sessions. You have made ample use of it regarding the debate we had together on 9 Ventôse Year 3 <note>February 27, 1795, in the French Republican Calendar. This was a famous public debate between the mystic philosopher Saint-Martin and the "Ideologue" Garat.</note>. You have rewritten almost from scratch, dismantled, and reconstructed several times the answers you are publishing today. You worked on these variations with a cool head; it is only fair that I undertake, also with a cool head, to present my replies to you. Although the proofs were shared with me at your invitation, I did not want to change or add anything to what I stated in the session; I only corrected a few intolerable errors that escaped the stenographers <note>The debates were recorded in shorthand by stenographers, a relatively new practice at the time to ensure transparency in public education.</note>. Besides, even if I had desired to do more, the printer would not have permitted it.

Thus, I have allowed to remain in these proofs the very short replies I made then to your observations on the three amendments recorded in my speech; although these replies are now out of place, sometimes even insignificant to the reader, because of the deletions and long interpolations <note>Later additions or insertions made to a text.</note> you have made.

It is not that I wish to make any complaint here; the public will no doubt believe that you will have

<meta>This page continues Louis-Claude de Saint-Martin’s critique of Citizen Garat’s edited version of their public debate at the Normal Schools. Saint-Martin expresses his frustration with the public format for discussing deep philosophical truths and the discrepancies between what was said in person and what was later written.</meta>

...said in the session everything he will have read in your writing. But you know how much, on this point, this will be far from the truth.

Moreover, I admit, even if you had elaborated more than you did in the assembly, the replies I made then would probably not have been any more extensive; either because our respective starting points were too distant, or because of the profound reluctance I have toward treating such great subjects before the public when time does not permit giving them all the development they would require. Such cases are matters for the office <note>original: *procès de bureau*; referring to administrative or legal matters settled through written reports and quiet study rather than oral argument</note>, and not matters for a public hearing <note>original: *procès d'audience*; a trial or debate held in open court</note>. And even now, since it is still before the public and only for a moment that we are to appear on stage, I take up the pen only with reluctance; regarding the hours we shall employ in our discussions as hours that are, roughly, wasted—for you, for me, and for our readers.

For you: because our mutual foundations are not level, and thus it is impossible for the building not to collapse at every moment.

For me: because, having been exhausted to the point of satiety by all these elementary questions which belong to the very lowest classes of instruction <note>Saint-Martin, a mystic, felt that the "Ideologue" philosophers were stuck on basic sensory questions while he had moved on to spiritual certainties.</note>, I regret returning to points which, for a long time now, have seemed settled to me.

<sig>H 3</sig>

<meta>This page continues a philosophical debate regarding the classification of human faculties, specifically addressing the definitions of "understanding" versus "moral sense" and "soul."</meta>

...of my intellectual and moral faculties, could compose that being you wish to call **understanding**; that which you reproach others for calling **conscience** (*page 33*) and me **moral sense**; but which you likely would not fear to call **soul**, **spirit**, **genius**, or **reason**, since these are the terms you use in various places—particularly on *page 29*—when speaking of Malebranche, whose errors you deplore even while paying tribute to his talents. <note>Nicolas Malebranche (1638–1715) was a prominent French rationalist philosopher; the author suggests the opponent is inconsistent in their terminology when praising him.</note>

Just as I am demanding regarding ideas, I am accommodating regarding words. In your program, you translated as "intelligence" what Bacon, your master, called *soul* <note>original: "anima"</note>. Another might, in turn, name as "soul" what you wish to call "intelligence." <note>Francis Bacon (1561–1626) was an English philosopher and a pioneer of the scientific method, often cited as an authority in Enlightenment debates.</note>

But by whatever name you designate this **moral faculty**, you can no longer deny its existence. You are no longer permitted to rely on your weak argument, which I challenged in my discourse (*page 9*), and which relied on the alleged necessity of having *two* moral senses: one to judge what is morally good, and another for what is morally bad. This is especially true since the example of our physical senses teaches us enough on this point; my sense of sight, though it is a single faculty, is sufficient for me to perceive with equal ease a man who has the misfortune of being a hunchback and a man who does not.

<summary>The author argues that whether one calls the human moral capacity "soul," "intelligence," or "moral sense," its existence is undeniable, refuting the opponent's claim that separate senses are needed to distinguish good from evil.</summary>
<keywords>moral sense, understanding, Nicolas Malebranche, Francis Bacon, soul vs intelligence, philosophical terminology, moral faculty</keywords>

--- Page 199 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's defense of the "moral sense," responding to a critic who insists on specific terminology. The author moves from arguing that the moral sense is a single faculty to explaining that various terms for the human mind are effectively interchangeable for his purposes.</meta>

It is therefore a matter of indifference, regarding this **moral faculty**, whether one calls it thought, **soul**, **reason**, **understanding**, **human instinct** (to distinguish it from animal instinct), **intelligence**, **heart**, **spirit**, or **conscience**; for me, all these things are but a single being, which I shall then consider from different angles, and according to whichever of its faculties happens to be predominant at the moment.

Thus, when I consider this being in relation to its moral affections or to what you call *moral notions*, I shall use the expression **moral faculty**, or even **moral sense**, because this expression then becomes unobjectionable as soon as its meaning is fixed. I shall consider, I say, within this moral faculty or this moral sense, that faculty within us which receives—through the organ of the senses, whether physical or intellectual—the true or false **impression** <note>In 18th and 19th-century philosophy, an "impression" is the immediate effect of an experience or thought upon the mind, before it is processed into a complex idea.</note> of what, according to the moral order, we call good and evil; a faculty in which my material senses are never the principle or the generating source of the impression it receives, even though they are often the organ and the conductors of it. They are like my windows, which transmit the light of the sun to my eye, but are by no means the principle of that light, which often reaches me without their help. <note>The "window" metaphor suggests that while our eyes and ears provide the data of the world, the "moral sense" possesses an internal light or capacity to judge that data independently of the physical organs.</note> It is a faculty which, as a consequence of the impression it receives, loves or hates the object that this impression presents to it; a faculty, finally, which draws near to or moves away from that object because of the attractive or repulsive affection that this impression creates within it for that object.

<summary>The author argues that various philosophical terms for the mind—such as soul, reason, and conscience—all refer to the same underlying human essence, and he defines the "moral sense" as the specific capacity that perceives and reacts to good and evil.</summary>
<keywords>moral faculty, moral sense, soul, human instinct, conscience, perception of good and evil, philosophical terminology, impressions</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author's philosophical exploration of the human mind, specifically distinguishing between the "understanding," which handles logical accuracy, and the "moral sense," which handles justice. When I consider this being under the character of the **understanding**, not only will I find it another seat <note>The "seat" refers to the metaphorical or physical location of a faculty within the human being.</note>, but I will even find it another use; and to be brief, I will limit myself to observing that the **understanding** judges **accuracy** <note>original: *justesse* — this refers to the logical correctness, precision, or "fittingness" of an idea.</note>, and that the **moral sense** judges **justice**; two things which among men have a different meaning, and even seem to have their separate departments, despite the mutual help they can provide each other in their respective administration.

You wish to explain the play of this **moral faculty** and all the operations of the **understanding** by means of **sensibility**. You have already seen ( *page 10 of my discourse* ) that I did not oppose this, because that name changed nothing in the core of things, and it only expressed the mode of the instruments and not the instruments themselves. Nevertheless, so as not to let you believe that I embrace your system, I readily acknowledge that everything is sensible <note>In this context, "sensible" means "capable of being perceived or felt" by the mind, rather than just "reasonable."</note> in all the operations of our **spirit** and our **soul**, or our **moral faculty**; but I deny that everything within them is **sensation**, because that word is reserved for the physical impressions of our matter, and by wanting to introduce it into a region of another order, you would tend to assimilate them, whereas you have already recognized their difference by admitting to me that **moral notions** were the exclusive domain of man alone.

Thus, do not believe that the **spiritualists** <note>Spiritualists here refers to philosophers who hold that the soul or mind is a distinct, non-material substance, contrasting with "materialists" who believe everything arises from physical matter.</note> are

Defeated by this confession, as you proclaim (*page 45.*)
The **spiritualists** <term>spiritualists: philosophers who maintain that the mind or spirit has a reality independent of physical matter</term>, while recognizing that **matter** has **sensations**, know that it does not think at all, as I demonstrated in my discourse (*page 9.*); although today you reject my proof somewhat hastily, as I will point out to you in a moment.

If they know that matter does not think, it is to this simple fact that they limit their statement; just as it was to this simple fact that my demonstration was limited.

They do not argue at all over whether our **sensations** are phenomena resulting from a certain **organization** of matter; and in this, they share your prudence and your moderation. Moreover, although they recognize the property of feeling within organized matter, they know that this property is only lent to it; and that when this matter is left to itself, it returns to its insignificance, if not to its nothingness <note>The author suggests that without the "spirit" to animate it, physical matter loses its sensory capabilities and becomes "null."</note>.

They do not argue among themselves over whether thought can or cannot ever be an attribute of organized matter, nor whether it is eternal, as you seem to believe I am among those who charge you with these opinions; regarding which opinions, however, I very willingly withdraw my accusation, if it were true that it had escaped me. But without me daring, from now on, to suspect you of being a **materialist** <term>materialist: someone who believes that nothing exists except matter and its movements, denying the existence of a separate soul or spirit</term>,

<meta>This page continues the author's defense of his philosophical position against a critic. It further distinguishes the "spiritualists" (proponents of the soul’s existence) from professional theologians and explores the specific argument regarding whether matter is capable of thought.</meta>

( 72 )

Since you affirm the contrary, I must admit that the **spiritualists** would not believe themselves so far from your **system** <note>original: "systême"; referring to the critic's philosophical framework, likely a form of sensationalism or materialism</note> if they were convinced, as you are, that all our thoughts are nothing but simple, abstract, or combined **sensations** (*page 44.*) They do not argue at all to clarify an infinity of questions which, most of the time, are only poorly posed; because they have their position already established on all of that, and they know the source of the misunderstandings and the fumbling that occur every day regarding these matters.

They are, since it must be said, too occupied with **positive truths** <term>positive truths: facts established through experience, observation, or "positive" evidence, rather than abstract speculation</term> to stop for vain disputes over the possibilities and the **contingence** <term>contingence: the philosophical concept that something is not necessarily one way, but could have been another</term> of things.

It is the **theologians** and the **metaphysicians** by profession who, having nothing more substantial to include in their deliberations, amuse themselves with these vague and resultless discussions. Now, the spiritualists are neither theologians nor metaphysicians by profession.

One can see from this moment why you reject (*page 47.*) my **demonstration** that **matter** does not think: it is mainly because you have added to my statement on the simple fact an extension that I had not given it regarding contingency, since I did not explain myself at all regarding the possibility or impossibility of another way of being for matter.

I know that as for **cultivation** <note>original: "culture"; in this context, it refers to the capacity for learning, education, or refinement in living beings</note>, you have not entirely determined the limit where this property stops in animals; you are persuaded that they acquire knowledge, and even arts (*page 20.*) Well then! <meta>This page continues the author’s rebuttal of his critic’s arguments. The previous page established that while "spiritualists" (those who believe the mind is separate from the body) acknowledge that matter has sensations, they deny that matter itself can think. Here, the author defends himself against the charge of circular reasoning by distinguishing between the mind’s active cultivation and the body’s passive sensations.</meta>

( 73 )

->Debates. Volume III. F<-

To this oversight, another was soon added, which was to give my proof a character of your own invention; after which you did not hesitate to proclaim yourself triumphant.

If I could have imagined that *not possessing cultivation of one's own* and *not feeling* were one and the same assertion, as you claim (page 47), I would have greatly contradicted myself. For, while refusing to matter the cultivation <note>The author uses "culture" (cultivation) here to mean the active, intellectual development of the mind, which he distinguishes from the passive physical reception of senses.</note> which it indeed does not possess on its own, I authentically grant it the sensations of which it is evidently the receptacle, the organ, and the focus. But you are so full of your "system of sensations" <term>system of sensations: likely a reference to Sensationalism, a philosophical theory (popularized by thinkers like Condillac) suggesting that all knowledge and mental faculties derive solely from sensory experience</term> that it will not be your fault if every word of our languages—if our entire dictionary, finally—is not one day reduced to the single word *to feel*. However, even if you had thus simplified language, you would not have, for all that, simplified the operations of living beings. Let us see, then, where this will lead us.

If cultivation is recognized as one of the rights of our spirit, and sensations are properties granted to matter, it is clear that even if—in the inaccurate account you give of my assertion—you were to reduce these two diverse operations to the single word *to feel*, it would no longer be I who should be accused (as you do) of offering the question itself, posed in different terms, as its own solution <note>The author is defending himself against the charge of "begging the question" or circular reasoning.</note>; it would be you who should be accused of providing two absolutely different things under the same term. But we have not yet finished.
