The only distinction that must be admitted lies in the various ways this moral sense is applied in these different examples.

This does not prevent this understanding itself, or what one might call the mind, regardless of the cause it defends, from being a necessary instrument in the order of reasoning, just as the moral sense is a necessary instrument in the order of affections. Thus, he who sees clearly in objects of discernment and he who sees falsely both prove that they possess a mind. Only, the first employs his mind with useful precision, and the other makes an use of it that is erroneous and more than useless.

<meta>This page continues the author’s philosophical investigation into the "moral sense." Having established that the mind is a necessary tool for reasoning, the author now argues that even intellectual "errors"—such as atheism or madness—serve as evidence of the mind’s existence, whereas the "moral sense" remains the driving force behind all human conviction, whether religious or skeptical.</meta>

( 78 )

<meta>visible bleed-through from reverse page</meta>

But if the latter did not possess this faculty of the mind, it would be as impossible for him to fall into errors of understanding as it would be for the former to apply his mind correctly, had he not possessed that mind or faculty for himself. In this sense, for anyone who reflects upon it, a madman and an atheist would prove the existence of this faculty of the mind in man just as clearly as the most well-ordered mind could do, even though the evidence points in opposite directions.

I am not speaking here of the idiot <note>original French: *l'imbécille*; in the early 19th-century context, this referred to someone with a profound intellectual disability or developmental delay where the mental faculties were seen as dormant</note>, in whom this faculty is suspended or delayed.

Returning then to my subject, I leave you the free choice either to incline toward the divine thing and toward the religious thing, as pious men do; or to declare an active war against both, like the impious; or simply to disdain them: whether your inclinations <note>original: *penchans*</note> carry you away, or the disorders they present to your mind fill you with indignation against them; whether you regard both as a truth or as a lie; whether you draw near to them or distance yourself from them; whatever side you take, it will always be your **moral sense** <note>The author uses "moral sense" to describe a fundamental human capacity for feeling and value that precedes logical argument</note> that will act either for or against; whether actively and by developing it, as pious and impious men do; or passively, by allowing it to be suppressed by the sight of the disorders that follow in their wake; or finally by allowing it to be damaged by your own inclinations.

And without it <note>Referring to the "moral sense" discussed on the previous page</note>, it will be impossible for you not only to reach a decision on any of these objects <note>In this philosophical context, "objects" refers to the subjects of contemplation, specifically the divine or religious matters</note>, but even to experience the slightest impression toward them, whether favorable or disadvantageous—just as it would be impossible for me to receive either a pleasant or unpleasant impression from the light of the sun if I were totally deprived of the sense of sight. <meta>This page continues the author’s response to critics who argue that we cannot know the internal lives of animals. The previous page established that if animals lack a "moral sense," they cannot perceive religious truths. Here, the author uses a "reductio ad absurdum" argument, suggesting that if we assume animals think and speak like humans, we must also imagine them having their own universities, pulpits, and religious rituals.</meta>

->( 80 )<-

furthermore, if they possessed all these means, why would they not make the same use of them among themselves as we do? One could not know if there were not among them conversations on spirituality and religious opinions just as there are among us, as well as those scholastic <note>Refers to "scholasticism," a method of critical thought and theological debate dominant in medieval universities</note> and philosophical discussions on lofty matters that occupy us so much and with so little fruit. And since we are making suppositions regarding these animals, I claim that one could just as easily suppose that they would not stop at simple conversations on these subjects, and that they would have the temptation, like us, to convert all these opinions—which they would stir up in their talks—into acts and ceremonies.

Thus, I could say to each of my opponents: it is impossible for you to judge what these animals do in this regard without your knowledge. For just as they are ignorant of what happens among us in that respect, we may well be ignorant of what happens among them. Thus, at the moment when you travel peacefully on your horse, at the moment when your dog catches a quail and your cat a mouse, perhaps some are in a sorbonne <note>original: "sorbonne"; a reference to the University of Paris, used here as a shorthand for a place of intense theological debate</note>, others in the pulpit, others performing the functions of dervishes <note>original: "derviches"; a term for members of Muslim religious orders, often used in 18th-century Europe to symbolize exotic religious devotion or ritual</note>, etc.

This is how risky suppositions lead us insensibly to the absurd. I return to my subject.
